You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given are a sequence of N positive integers A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N and another positive integer S.

For a non-empty subset T of the set \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}, let us define f(T) as follows:

 - f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets \{x_1, x_2, \ldots , x_k \} of T such that A_{x_1}+A_{x_2}+\cdots +A_{x_k} = S.
Find the sum of f(T) over all 2^N-1 subsets T of \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}. Since the sum can be enormous, print it modulo 998244353.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq S \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq A_i \leq 3000

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N S
A_1 A_2 ... A_N

-----Output-----
Print the sum of f(T) modulo 998244353.

-----Sample Input-----
3 4
2 2 4

-----Sample Output-----
6

For each T, the value of f(T) is shown below. The sum of these values is 6.
 - f(\{1\}) = 0
 - f(\{2\}) = 0
 - f(\{3\}) = 1 (One subset \{3\} satisfies the condition.)
 - f(\{1, 2\}) = 1 (\{1, 2\})
 - f(\{2, 3\}) = 1 (\{3\})
 - f(\{1, 3\}) = 1 (\{3\})
 - f(\{1, 2, 3\}) = 2 (\{1, 2\}, \{3\})

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the sum of f(T) over all non-empty subsets T of {1, 2, ..., N}, where f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets of T that sum to S.

In other words, for each possible subset T, we count how many of its non-empty subsets have sum exactly S, and then sum this count over all possible T.

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating through all subsets T and counting valid subsets within them, we can change our perspective. 

For each possible non-empty subset of indices that sums to S, we can count in how many supersets T this subset can appear.

If we have a non-empty subset of indices that sums to S, say {i₁, i₂, ..., iₖ}, then this subset will contribute to f(T) for every superset T that contains all these indices.

For a fixed subset of indices that sums to S, how many supersets T contain it?

- For each element j not in the subset {i₁, i₂, ..., iₖ}, we have two choices: include it in T or not include it in T.
- So if there are (N - k) elements not in the subset, then there are 2^(N-k) supersets T that contain this subset.

Therefore, our answer is the sum over all non-empty subsets of indices that sum to S of 2^(N-k), where k is the size of the subset.

## Approach

1. Use dynamic programming to count the number of ways to form subsets with sum S
2. But we also need to track the size of the subset to compute 2^(N-k) for each subset
3. We can use a DP where dp[i][s] represents the sum of 2^(N-k) for all subsets of first i elements that sum to s, where k is the size of the subset

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we need to be more careful.

Let me define dp[i][s] as the sum of 2^(N - k) over all subsets of the first i elements that sum to s, where k is the size of the subset.

But when we add a new element A_i, we have two choices: include it or not include it.

If we don't include it, the contribution to dp[i][s] is the same as dp[i-1][s].

If we include it, then we're adding a subset of size k+1 with sum s + A_i. The contribution would be 2^(N - (k+1)) = 2^(N-k-1).

So the recurrence is:
- dp[i][s] = dp[i-1][s] + (contribution when including A_i)

When including A_i, if we have a subset of size k that sums to s' and we add A_i to get sum s = s' + A_i, then the contribution is 2^(N- (k+1)) = 2^(N-k-1).

But we don't track k in the state. We need to track the size.

Alternative approach: Let dp[i][s] be the sum of 2^(N-k) for all subsets of the first i elements that sum to s.

When we add element i with value A_i:
- Don't take A_i: contribution is dp[i-1][s]
- Take A_i: for each way to form sum s' with previous elements, we form sum s = s' + A_i with size increased by 1. The contribution is 2^(N - (k+1)) where k was the size before.

But we don't know k. The issue is that 2^(N-k-1) depends on k.

Actually, notice that when we add an element with sum target s, the contribution when including it is exactly half of the previous contribution for sum s - A_i, because:
- When we include A_i, we're essentially taking each subset of previous elements with sum s - A_i and adding A_i
- If such a subset had size k, then after adding A_i, the new subset has size k+1, so its contribution is 2^(N-k-1)
- The contribution from subsets of the first i-1 elements with sum s - A_i would be dp[i-1][s-A_i] 
- But each of these contributions was 2^(N-k) for size k, so when we convert to size k+1, we get 2^(N-k-1) = (1/2) * 2^(N-k)

Therefore, the contribution from including A_i is exactly (1/2) * dp[i-1][s-A_i]

```python
mod = 998244353

def solve():
    N, S = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Initialize dp: dp[s] represents the sum of 2^(N-k) for all subsets of processed elements that sum to s
    # Initially, only the empty subset with sum 0
    dp = [0] * (S + 1)
    dp[0] = pow(2, N, mod)  # empty subset: size 0, contribution 2^(N-0)
    
    # For each element in A
    for a in A:
        # Create new_dp for the next state
        new_dp = dp[:]  # copy current state (don't take current element)
        
        # For each sum s from a to S, add the contribution of including current element
        for s in range(a, S + 1):
            # When we include element a, we take all subsets that sum to s-a and add a
            # Each such subset had contribution 2^(N-k), and after adding, size increases by 1, so new contribution is 2^(N-k-1) = (1/2) * 2^(N-k)
            # So we add inv2 * dp[s - a]
            inv2 = pow(2, mod - 2, mod)
            new_dp[s] = (new_dp[s] + inv2 * dp[s - a]) % mod
            
        dp = new_dp
    
    print(dp[S])

solve()
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
mod = 998244353

def solve():
    N, S = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Initialize dp: dp[s] represents the sum of 2^(N-k) for all subsets of processed elements that sum to s
    # Initially, only the empty subset with sum 0
    dp = [0] * (S + 1)
    dp[0] = pow(2, N, mod)  # empty subset: size 0, contribution 2^(N-0)
    
    # For each element in A
    for a in A:
        # Create new_dp for the next state
        new_dp = dp[:]  # copy current state (don't take current element)
        
        # For each sum s from a to S, add the contribution of including current element
        for s in range(a, S + 1):
            # When we include element a, we take all subsets that sum to s-a and add a
            # Each such subset had contribution 2^(N-k), and after adding, size increases by 1, so new contribution is 2^(N-k-1) = (1/2) * 2^(N-k)
            # So we add inv2 * dp[s - a]
            inv2 = pow(2, mod - 2, mod)
            new_dp[s] = (new_dp[s] + inv2 * dp[s - a]) % mod
            
        dp = new_dp
    
    print(dp[S])

solve()

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the sum of f(T) over all non-empty subsets T of {1, 2, ..., N}, where f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets of T that sum to S.

In other words, for each possible subset T of the original array, we count how many non-empty subsets of T sum to S, then sum this count over all possible T.

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating through all possible subsets T and then counting valid subsets within each T, we can use a different approach by considering contribution of each possible subset that sums to S.

Let's think about it this way: A subset {i₁, i₂, ..., i_k} that sums to S will contribute to f(T) for every subset T that contains all elements of this subset.

For a specific subset of indices (say {i₁, i₂, ..., i_k}) that sums to S, how many T's contain this subset? 

Well, T must contain all the indices in {i₁, i₂, ..., i_k}, and can contain any subset of the remaining N-k indices. So there are 2^(N-k) such subsets T.

Therefore, the total sum is the sum over all non-empty subsets of indices that sum to S of 2^(N-k), where k is the size of the subset.

## Algorithm

1. Find all non-empty subsets of indices that sum to S
2. For each such subset of size k, add 2^(N-k) to the result
3. Return result modulo 998244353

However, enumerating all subsets directly would be too slow (up to 2^N subsets). We need a dynamic programming approach.

## Dynamic Programming Approach

We can use DP where dp[i][j] represents the number of ways to achieve sum j using the first i elements, considering the contribution from each subset.

But we need to account for the fact that each valid subset contributes 2^(N-k) where k is its size.

Alternative approach: Think about the contribution of each element to different subset sizes.

Actually, let me reconsider. For each valid subset that sums to S, its contribution is 2^(N - k) where k is its size.

We can use a DP that tracks both the sum and the number of elements used.

But we need the contribution of each subset of size k. So we can do:

Let dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j using exactly k elements.

Then the total answer is sum over all subsets with sum S of (2^(N-k) * dp[S][k]).

We can compute this with a 2D DP where dp[sum][count] represents the number of subsets of size 'count' that sum to 'sum'.

## Implementation Steps

1. Initialize dp[0][0] = 1 (empty subset with sum 0 and count 0)
2. For each element A_i:
   - Create a new dp state by either including or excluding A_i
   - When including, we add to dp[sum + A_i][count + 1]
3. After processing all elements, sum over all k from 1 to N: dp[S][k] * 2^(N-k)

Wait, this gives us the total number of subsets that sum to S, multiplied by their size contributions. But actually, each subset of size k contributes exactly 2^(N-k).

So yes, the answer is: sum_{k=1}^{N} (dp[S][k] * 2^(N-k))

Let me verify with the sample:
- N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]
- Valid subsets that sum to 4: {1,2}, {3} (since A1=2, A2=2, A3=4)
- Size 2: {1,2}, contributes 2^(3-2) = 2^1 = 2
- Size 1: {3}, contributes 2^(3-1) = 2^2 = 4
- Total: 2 + 4 = 6 ✓

This matches the expected output.

## Optimization

The constraints are N, S ≤ 3000, so we can use a DP with dimensions (S+1) × (N+1).

We need to handle modulo 998244353.

Also, we need to precompute powers of 2 up to 2^N modulo 998244353.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    # Read input
    N, S = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Precompute powers of 2 up to N
    pow2 = [1] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD
    
    # dp[sum][count] = number of ways to achieve sum with exactly count elements
    # We only need sum from 0 to S
    dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
    dp[0][0] = 1  # empty subset
    
    # Process each element
    for a in A:
        # We need to update dp in reverse order to avoid using updated values
        new_dp = [row[:] for row in dp]  # copy current state
        
        for sum_val in range(S + 1):
            for count in range(N):
                if dp[sum_val][count] == 0:
                    continue
                new_sum = sum_val + a
                if new_sum <= S:
                    new_count = count + 1
                    if new_count <= N:
                        new_dp[new_sum][new_count] = (new_dp[new_sum][new_count] + dp[sum_val][count]) % MOD
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Calculate result
    result = 0
    for k in range(1, N + 1):  # k from 1 to N (non-empty subsets)
        if dp[S][k] > 0:
            result = (result + dp[S][k] * pow2[N - k]) % MOD
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with the DP update. When we process each element, we're updating the dp table, but the way I'm doing it (copying and then updating) might not be correct because we need to be careful about the order.

Actually, the standard approach for subset sum with size constraint is to iterate backwards to avoid using updated values in the same iteration. Let me fix this:

```python
MOD = 998244353

# Read input
N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute powers of 2 up to N
pow2 = [1] * (N + 1)
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD

# dp[sum][count] = number of ways to achieve sum with exactly count elements
dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
dp[0][0] = 1

# Process each element
for a in A:
    # Go backwards to avoid using updated values in the same iteration
    for sum_val in range(S, -1, -1):
        for count in range(N, 0, -1):
            if sum_val >= a:
                dp[sum_val][count] = (dp[sum_val][count] + dp[sum_val - a][count - 1]) % MOD

# Calculate result
result = 0
for k in range(1, N + 1):
    result = (result + dp[S][k] * pow2[N - k]) % MOD

print(result)
```

Wait, this still has a bug. The issue is that when we update dp[sum_val][count], we're modifying the state that might be used in the same iteration. But in our backward iteration, we're processing from high sum_val to low, and from high count to low, so if we update dp[sum_val][count] using dp[sum_val-a][count-1], that hasn't been updated yet in this iteration.

However, in our current code, we're not actually updating from the previous state correctly. The standard way is to iterate backwards and update based on the previous state.

Actually, the current code has the correct approach: we're going backwards so that when we compute dp[sum_val][count], we're using the state from before processing this element a.

But in my first version, I was doing a full copy, which is more expensive. The backward iteration with updating in place is the standard approach.

Let me trace through the sample:
- N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]
- Start: dp[0][0]=1, all others 0
- Process a=2:
  - For sum_val from 4 to 0, count from N to 1:
  - sum_val=4: sum_val>=2, so dp[4][1] += dp[2][0] = 0
  - sum_val=2: dp[2][1] += dp[0][0] = 1
  - So after a=2: dp[2][1]=1
- Process a=2:
  - sum_val=4: dp[4][1] += dp[2][0]=0, dp[4][2] += dp[2][1]=1 → dp[4][2]=1
  - sum_val=2: dp[2][1] += dp[0][0]=1 → dp[2][1]=1+1=2
  - sum_val=0: nothing
  - So after processing second 2: dp[4][2]=1, dp[2][1]=2
- Process a=4:
  - sum_val=4: dp[4][1] += dp[0][0]=1 → dp[4][1]=1
  - sum_val=2: dp[2][1] += dp[-2][0] (invalid) → skip
  - So after processing a=4: dp[4][1]=1, dp[4][2]=1
- Result: for k=1: dp[4][1]*2^(3-1) = 1 * 4 = 4
- for k=2: dp[4][2]*2^(3-2) = 1 * 2 = 2
- Total: 4+2 = 6 ✓

Perfect! This matches.

```python
N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# dp[s][k] = number of ways to achieve sum s using exactly k elements
# We'll use 0-indexed arrays: s from 0 to S, k from 0 to N

dp[0][0] = 1

    # We need to update dp in reverse order to avoid using the same element twice
    # We create a new state or update backwards
    new_dp = [row[:] for row in dp]  # copy current state
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the sum of f(T) over all non-empty subsets T of {1, 2, ..., N}, where f(T) is the number of non-empty subsets of T that sum to S.

In other words, for each possible subset T, we count how many non-empty subsets of T sum to S, and we sum this count across all possible T.

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating through all subsets T and then counting their valid subsubsets, we can use a different approach by considering which specific subset summing to S contributes to the final answer.

For any subset X of {1, 2, ..., N} that sums to S, we need to count in how many different T (supersets of X) this subset X appears as a subset.

For a fixed subset X that sums to S, X will be a subset of T if and only if T contains all elements of X. For each element in X, it must be in T, and for each element not in X, it can be either in or out of T.

So if X has k elements, then there are 2^(N-k) possible T's that contain X as a subset.

Therefore, the total contribution of subset X (which sums to S) to the final answer is 2^(N-|X|).

## Algorithm

1. Find all non-empty subsets of indices that sum to S (from the array A)
2. For each such subset X with size k, add 2^(N-k) to the answer
3. Return the answer modulo 998244353

## Optimization

Instead of generating all subsets (which would be exponential), we can use dynamic programming to count the contribution of all subsets that sum to S.

We can use DP where dp[i][j] represents the total contribution from considering first i elements and achieving sum j.

But we need to track the size of the subset as well, since we need 2^(N-k) where k is the size.

Actually, we can reframe: For each possible subset that sums to S, we want to add 2^(N - size_of_subset).

We can use DP where dp[j][k] = sum of contributions (i.e., 2^(N-k)) for all subsets of first i elements that have sum j and size k.

But this might be too memory intensive.

Alternative approach: We can use generating functions or iterate through all possible subsets using bit manipulation, but with N up to 3000, we can't iterate through all 2^3000 subsets.

We need a more efficient DP.

Let me think again: We want to compute the sum over all subsets X (that sum to S) of 2^(N-|X|).

This equals 2^N × (sum over all subsets X that sum to S of 2^(-|X|)).

So we can compute: Sum_{X: sum(X)=S} 2^(-|X|) and multiply by 2^N.

But 2^(-|X|) is not an integer, so this might not be clean.

Wait, actually, 2^(N-|X|) = 2^N / 2^|X|, so the total sum is 2^N × sum_{X: sum(X)=S} 1/2^|X|.

But working with fractions is messy in modular arithmetic.

Let me go back to: For each subset X that sums to S, we add 2^(N-|X|).

We can use dynamic programming where dp[i][j] represents the sum of 2^(N-k) for all subsets of first i elements that have sum j and size k.

But we need to track both sum and size, and the size affects the power of 2.

We can define dp[j][k] = sum of contributions (which is 2^(N-k)) for all subsets (from any elements) that achieve sum j and have size k.

But the contribution depends on the final size, so we need to build up the solution.

Actually, let's define dp[j][k] = sum of 2^(N-k) for all ways to achieve sum j with k elements.

But how do we update this?

When we add a new element with value a, for each existing state (j, k), we can either:
- Not take it: state remains (j, k)
- Take it: new state becomes (j+a, k+1) with contribution 2^(N-(k+1)) = 2^(N-k-1) = (2^(N-k)) / 2

Wait, this is getting complex with fractions.

Alternative idea: Instead of tracking the final contribution, track the sum of 2^(N-k) for each state.

But the recurrence will involve division by 2.

In modular arithmetic, division by 2 is multiplication by the modular inverse of 2.

Since we're working modulo 998244353, and 2 has an inverse, we can do this.

Let me define dp[i][j][k] = sum of contributions for subsets using elements from first i elements, sum j, size k.

Then:
- Initially: dp[0][0][0] = 1 (empty set with sum 0, size 0, contribution 2^(N-0) = 2^N)
- For each element a[i], we update:
  - dp[i+1][j][k] += dp[i][j][k]  (not take a[i])
  - dp[i+1][j+a[i]][k+1] += dp[i][j][k] * inv(2)  (take a[i], since contribution becomes 2^(N-(k+1)) = 2^(N-k)/2)

The key insight: When we add an element, if we already have a contribution of C for a subset of size k, then when we add this element, the new subset has size k+1 and contribution 2^(N-(k+1)) = 2^(N-k-1) = (2^(N-k)) / 2 = C / 2.

So we multiply by the modular inverse of 2.

We need to work with modular arithmetic, and we can precompute powers of 2 modulo 998244353.

But note: our DP state doesn't need to go beyond sum S and size N.

The maximum sum we care about is S, and maximum size is N.

So we can do:

- Initialize a 2D DP array dp[j][k] for j in [0,S] and k in [0,N]
- dp[0][0] = 1, and all others 0 initially? Wait no.

Actually, our initial state is the empty set with sum 0, size 0, and contribution 2^N.

But we want all subsets that sum to S, so we'll build up the DP.

Let me clarify the initial state:

Before processing any elements, we have the empty set. Its contribution is 2^(N-0) = 2^N.

But the empty set itself doesn't sum to S (unless S=0, but S≥1), so we don't care about it directly.

However, when we add elements, we're building up all possible subsets.

So let's define dp[j][k] = sum of 2^(N-k) over all subsets (using some of the elements) that have sum j and size k.

Initially (before any elements), dp[0][0] = 2^N (the empty set)

Then for each element a[i] from 1 to N:
  - Create a new dp array (or update in reverse order)
  - For each current state (j, k), we can:
    - Not take a[i]: state (j, k) remains
    - Take a[i]: state (j+a[i], k+1) gets dp[j][k] * inv(2)

After processing all N elements, we sum up all dp[S][k] for k from 1 to N (since non-empty subsets).

Wait, but the empty set (sum 0, size 0) is included in dp[0][0], which is 2^N, but we only care about subsets that sum to S.

Also, for a subset to sum to S, it must be non-empty? The problem says "non-empty subsets", so yes.

But in our DP, we count all subsets (including those of size 0), so when we sum dp[S][k] for k≥1, we exclude the empty set from sum S=0.

Actually, when S > 0, the empty set doesn't contribute to sum S, so we don't need to worry about k=0 for sum S>0.

So the answer is simply dp[S][k] for k from 1 to N, summed up.

But let me verify with the sample:
N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]

First, compute 2^N = 8 (mod 998244353)

Initial state: dp[0][0] = 8

Process element 1 with value 2:
- Not take: dp[0][0] = 8
- Take: dp[2][1] += 8 * inv(2) = 8 * 499122177 = 4 (since 2*499122177 ≡ 1 mod 998244353)

Now dp[0][0]=8, dp[2][1]=4

Process element 2 with value 2:
- Not take: dp[0][0]=8, dp[2][1]=4
- Take: 
  - From (0,0): add 8 * inv(2) = 4 to dp[2][1] → dp[2][1] becomes 4+4=8
  - From (2,1): add 4 * inv(2) = 2 to dp[4][2] → dp[4][2] = 2

Now: dp[0][0]=8, dp[2][1]=8, dp[4][2]=2

Process element 3 with value 4:
- Not take: all states remain
- Take:
  - From (0,0): add 8 * inv(2) = 4 to dp[4][1]
  - From (2,1): add 8 * inv(2) = 4 to dp[6][2]
  - From (4,2): add 2 * inv(2) = 1 to dp[8][3]

Now after processing all:
- dp[4][1] = 4
- dp[4][2] = 2 (from previous)
- dp[4][3] = 0

So total contribution for sum 4: dp[4][1] + dp[4][2] = 4 + 2 = 6

This matches the expected output!

The contribution from size 1: {3} with value 4 → one subset
The contribution from size 2: {1,2} with values 2+2=4 → one subset

But according to our calculation, dp[4][1]=4 and dp[4][2]=2... why 4?

Wait, the subsets that lead to sum 4:
- Size 1: only one subset: {3} which has value 4
- Size 2: only one subset: {1,2} which has values 2+2=4

But we got dp[4][1]=4 and dp[4][2]=2.

Let me trace:
- When we take element 3 (value 4) with state (0,0): this represents the empty set. Taking it gives subset {3} with sum 4, size 1. Contribution should be 2^(3-1) = 2^2 = 4. Yes! The contribution is 4.
- When we take element 3 with state (2,1): this is subset {1} alone (first element). Taking element 3 gives {1,3} with sum 2+4=6, not 4. I made a mistake.

Wait, in our DP, when we process element 3 with value 4, from state (2,1), we add to dp[6][2], not dp[4][2].

From state (4,2): we take element 3 to get sum 8, not 4.

So only state (0,0) gives us a new sum 4 with size 1.

But where does dp[4][2] come from?

Earlier: after processing elements 1 and 2, we had dp[4][2]=2. This comes from taking first element 1 (value 2) and element 2 (value 2). This gives subset {1,2} with sum 4, size 2. The contribution was 2^(3-2) = 2^1 = 2. Correct.

When we process element 3, we don't create any new sum 4 with size 2 by taking element 3 with existing sum 4 (since 4+4=8).

So what about the subset {3} with value 4? It comes from taking element 3 with the empty set (sum 0).

In our DP, when we process element 3:
- From (0,0): we add 8 * inv(2) = 4 to dp[4][1]

And we already have from previous steps dp[4][2]=2 (from {1,2}).

So total for sum 4: 4 + 2 = 6.

This matches! The 4 comes from subset {3} being a valid subset of T for various T. 

For subset {3}: it appears in any T that contains element 3. There are 2^(3-1) = 4 such T (since elements 1 and 2 can be in or out).

Similarly, for subset {1,2}: it appears in any T that contains elements 1 and 2. There are 2^(3-2) = 2 such T (element 3 can be in or out).

So total: 4 + 2 = 6.

Perfect!

## Implementation

Steps:
1. Precompute modular inverse of 2 modulo 998244353
2. Initialize dp[j][k] = 0 for all j,k, with dp[0][0] = 2^N
3. For each element a[i] in A:
   - Create a new dp array (or update in reverse order to avoid using updated values)
   - For each current sum j from 0 to S:
     - For each current size k from 0 to N:
       - If dp[j][k] > 0:
         - Increment dp[j][k] (not taking a[i])
         - Add dp[j][k] * inv2 to dp[j+a[i]][k+1] (taking a[i]), but only if j+a[i] <= S
4. After processing all elements, sum dp[S][k] for k from 1 to N

Note: We use reverse iteration for j and k to avoid overwriting values we still need.

Actually, we can update in reverse order of j and k to avoid interference.

But since we're adding to higher indices, and we're iterating from high to low, we can update in reverse order of j and k.

Alternatively, we can make a copy of the current dp state.

Given constraints (S, N ≤ 3000), we can use a 2D array of size (S+1) x (N+1).

Let me implement:

```python
MOD = 998244353

def pow_mod(base, exp, mod):
    result = 1
    base = base % mod
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = (result * base) % mod
        exp = exp >> 1
        base = (base * base) % mod
    return result

def mod_inverse(a, mod):
    return pow_mod(a, mod-2, mod)

# Read input
N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Precompute 2^N mod MOD
two_power_N = pow_mod(2, N, MOD)

# Modular inverse of 2
inv2 = mod_inverse(2, MOD)

# DP: dp[j][k] = sum of contributions for subsets with sum j and size k
# Initially, only empty set: sum 0, size 0, contribution 2^N
dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
dp[0][0] = two_power_N

# Process each element
for a in A:
    # Create new dp state based on current dp
    # We'll update in reverse order to avoid using updated values
    new_dp = [row[:] for row in dp]  # copy current state
    
    # For each existing state, if we can take the element a
    for j in range(S + 1):
        for k in range(N + 1):
            if dp[j][k] == 0:
                continue
            # Take the element a
            new_sum = j + a
            if new_sum <= S:
                new_size = k + 1
                if new_size <= N:
                    new_dp[new_sum][new_size] = (new_dp[new_sum][new_size] + dp[j][k] * inv2) % MOD
    
    # Update dp to new_dp
    dp = new_dp

# Sum all contributions for subsets that sum to S and have size >= 1
result = 0
for k in range(1, N + 1):
    result = (result + dp[S][k]) % MOD

print(result)
```

Wait, in the above, when we take an element, we're adding dp[j][k] * inv2 to new_dp[new_sum][new_size].

But in the initialization, dp[0][0] = 2^N, which is the contribution of the empty set.

When we take elements, we build up the contributions.

But is the contribution correctly calculated?

Yes: when we take an element, we're multiplying by inv2 because the size increases by 1, so the contribution 2^(N-k) becomes 2^(N-(k+1)) = 2^(N-k)/2.

And when we don't take the element, the contribution remains unchanged.

This matches the recurrence.

Let me test with the small example manually.

N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]

two_power_N = 2^3 = 8
inv2 = 499122177 (since 2*499122177 ≡ 1 mod 998244353)

Initial: dp[0][0] = 8

Process a=2:
- From (0,0): new_sum=2, new_size=1: add 8 * inv2 = 4 to dp[2][1]
- After update: dp[0][0]=8, dp[2][1]=4

Process a=2:
- From (0,0): add 8*inv2=4 to dp[2][1] → dp[2][1] becomes 4+4=8
- From (2,1): add 4*inv2=2 to dp[4][2] → dp[4][2]=2
- After update: dp[0][0]=8, dp[2][1]=8, dp[4][2]=2

Process a=4:
- From (0,0): add 8*inv2=4 to dp[4][1] → dp[4][1]=4
- From (2,1): add 8*inv2=4 to dp[6][2] → dp[6][2]=4
- From (4,2): add 2*inv2=1 to dp[8][3] → dp[8][3]=1
- So dp[4][1]=4, dp[4][2]=2

Final sum: 4 + 2 = 6 ✓

Perfect.

One more thing: what if S=0? But constraints say S≥1, so we don't need to handle it.

Also, we're only summing for k≥1, which is correct since non-empty subsets.

Therefore, the solution is correct.

For a non-empty subset T of the set \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}, let us define f(T) as follows:

 - f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets \{x_1, x_2, \ldots , x_k \} of T such that A_{x_1}+A_{x_2}+\cdots +A_{x_k} = S.
Find the sum of f(T) over all 2^N-1 subsets T of \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}. Since the sum can be enormous, print it modulo 998244353.

 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq S \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq A_i \leq 3000

 - f(\{1, 3\}) = 1 (\{3\})

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the sum of f(T) over all non-empty subsets T of {1, 2, ..., N}, where f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets of T that sum to S.

For each subset T, we count how many of its non-empty subsets have sum exactly S, then sum this count over all possible T.

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating through all subsets T and then counting valid subsets within each T, we can use linearity of expectation or double counting.

Let me think about this differently: For each possible way to form a subset of elements that sums to S, how many total times does this particular subset contribute to the final sum?

Actually, let's think about when a specific subset U (which sums to S) is counted in f(T).

A subset U will be counted in f(T) if and only if:
- U ⊆ T (all elements of U are in T)
- T is non-empty
- U is non-empty (which is given since it sums to S)

So for a fixed subset U with sum S, it contributes to f(T) for every non-empty subset T such that U ⊆ T.

If U has k elements, then T can contain any subset of the remaining (N-k) elements, plus all elements of U. So there are 2^(N-k) such T that contain U.

However, we also need to ensure T is non-empty. Since U is non-empty, any T containing U is automatically non-empty.

So each subset U that has sum exactly S contributes exactly 2^(N-|U|) to the total sum.

Therefore, our answer is: Σ (over all non-empty subsets U of {1,2,...,N} with sum S) of 2^(N-|U|)

We can restructure this: for each possible size k, we can count how many subsets of size k have sum S, and each such subset contributes 2^(N-k).

## Algorithm

1. Use dynamic programming to count the number of subsets of each size that sum to S.
2. For each size k from 1 to N, if there are ways to form a subset of size k with sum S, add (count of such subsets) × 2^(N-k) to the answer.
3. Return the result modulo 998244353.

We can use DP where dp[i][j][k] represents the number of ways to choose a subset from first i elements with sum j and size k.

But note that N and S are up to 3000, so we need to be careful about time complexity.

Actually, we can use a 2D DP: dp[j][k] = number of ways to form sum j using k elements.

We initialize dp[0][0] = 1 (empty set), and then for each element A_i, we update the DP table by considering whether to include A_i or not.

However, note that we need to track sum and count, and the total number of elements is limited by N.

## Implementation Steps

1. Initialize a 2D array dp with dimensions (S+1) × (N+1), all zeros.
2. dp[0][0] = 1 (one way to make sum 0 with 0 elements)
3. For each element A_i in the array:
   - Create a new state or update backwards to avoid using the same element twice
   - For sum from S down to 0, and for size from N down to 0:
     - dp[new_sum][new_size] += dp[old_sum][old_size] if new_sum = old_sum + A_i and new_size = old_size + 1
4. After processing all elements, sum over all k from 1 to N: dp[S][k] × 2^(N-k)

Wait, let me reconsider the physics: when we have a subset U of size k that sums to S, it contributes 2^(N-k) to the total.

So we need to compute: Σ_{k=1}^{N} (number of k-element subsets with sum S) × 2^(N-k)

But note: we need to consider all possible subsets, not just those of a specific size.

Let me verify with the sample:
- N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]

Subsets that sum to 4:
- {3} with sum 4 (size 1)
- {1,2} with sum 2+2=4 (size 2)

So:
- For size 1: one subset (element 3) → contributes 2^(3-1) = 4
- For size 2: one subset (elements 1,2) → contributes 2^(3-2) = 2
- Total = 4 + 2 = 6 ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm is:
1. Use DP to count the number of subsets of each size that have sum exactly S.
2. For each size k from 1 to N, if count[k] > 0, add count[k] * (2^(N-k)) to answer.

But note: the empty set has sum 0 and size 0, but we don't count it since we need non-empty subsets.

Our DP should naturally exclude the empty set from the sum S (since we start with dp[0][0]=1 and build up).

Let me implement the DP:

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    N, S = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j using exactly k elements
    # We only need j from 0 to S, k from 0 to N
    dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
    dp[0][0] = 1
    
    # Process each element
    for a in A:
        # Process backwards to avoid using the same element multiple times
        new_dp = [row[:] for row in dp]  # copy current state
        for j in range(S, -1, -1):
            for k in range(N):
                if dp[j][k] > 0:
                    new_sum = j + a
                    if new_sum <= S:
                        new_dp[new_sum][k + 1] = (new_dp[new_sum][k + 1] + dp[j][k]) % MOD
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Now sum over all k from 1 to N: dp[S][k] * 2^(N-k)
    result = 0
    # Precompute powers of 2 up to N
    pow2 = [1] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD
    
    for k in range(1, N + 1):
        if dp[S][k] > 0:
            result = (result + dp[S][k] * pow2[N - k]) % MOD
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

Wait, I don't need to use new_dp when copying. I can update backwards to avoid reusing elements.

Actually, let me fix the DP to be more standard:

```python
MOD = 998244353

N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j using exactly k elements
dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
dp[0][0] = 1

for a in A:
    # Update backwards to avoid using the same element twice
    # We go from high sum to low sum and high size to low size
    for j in range(S, -1, -1):
        for k in range(N, 0, -1):
            if dp[j][k-1] > 0:
                new_sum = j + a
                if new_sum <= S:
                    dp[new_sum][k] = (dp[new_sum][k] + dp[j][k-1]) % MOD

# Now sum over all k from 1 to N: dp[S][k] * 2^(N-k)
result = 0
# Precompute powers of 2
pow2 = [1] * (N + 1)
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD

for k in range(1, N + 1):
    result = (result + dp[S][k] * pow2[N - k]) % MOD

print(result)
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j using exactly k elements
dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
dp[0][0] = 1

for a in A:
    # Update backwards to avoid using the same element multiple times
    # Go from high sum to low sum, high size to low size
    for j in range(S, -1, -1):
        for k in range(N, 0, -1):
            if dp[j][k-1] > 0:
                new_sum = j + a
                if new_sum <= S:
                    dp[new_sum][k] = (dp[new_sum][k] + dp[j][k-1]) % MOD

# Now sum over all k from 1 to N: dp[S][k] * 2^(N-k)
result = 0
# Precompute powers of 2 up to N
pow2 = [1] * (N + 1)
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD

for k in range(1, N + 1):
    result = (result + dp[S][k] * pow2[N - k]) % MOD

print(result)
```

```python
MOD = 998244353

N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j using exactly k elements
dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
dp[0][0] = 1

for a in A:
    # Update backwards to avoid using the same element multiple times
    for j in range(S, -1, -1):
        for k in range(N, 0, -1):
            if dp[j][k-1] > 0:
                new_sum = j + a
                if new_sum <= S:
                    dp[new_sum][k] = (dp[new_sum][k] + dp[j][k-1]) % MOD

# Now sum over all k from 1 to N: dp[S][k] * 2^(N-k)
result = 0
# Precompute powers of 2 up to N
pow2 = [1] * (N + 1)
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    pow2[i] = (pow2[i-1] * 2) % MOD

for k in range(1, N + 1):
    result = (result + dp[S][k] * pow2[N - k]) % MOD
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Given are a sequence of N positive integers A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N and another positive integer S.

For a non-empty subset T of the set \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}, let us define f(T) as follows:

 - f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets \{x_1, x_2, \ldots , x_k \} of T such that A_{x_1}+A_{x_2}+\cdots +A_{x_k} = S.
Find the sum of f(T) over all 2^N-1 subsets T of \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}. Since the sum can be enormous, print it modulo 998244353.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq S \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq A_i \leq 3000

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N S
A_1 A_2 ... A_N

-----Output-----
Print the sum of f(T) modulo 998244353.

-----Sample Input-----
3 4
2 2 4

-----Sample Output-----
6

For each T, the value of f(T) is shown below. The sum of these values is 6.
 - f(\{1\}) = 0
 - f(\{2\}) = 0
 - f(\{3\}) = 1 (One subset \{3\} satisfies the condition.)
 - f(\{1, 2\}) = 1 (\{1, 2\})
 - f(\{2, 3\}) = 1 (\{3\})
 - f(\{1, 3\}) = 1 (\{3\})
 - f(\{1, 2, 3\}) = 2 (\{1, 2\}, \{3\})

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the sum of f(T) over all non-empty subsets T of {1, 2, ..., N}, where f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets of T that sum to S.

In other words, for each non-empty subset T of the original set, we count how many non-empty subsets of T sum to S, then sum all these counts.

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating over all possible subsets T and then counting valid subsets within T, we can use a different approach by considering each possible combination of elements that sum to S, and counting in how many ways this combination can appear as a subset of some T.

For any subset of elements that sums to S, say {i₁, i₂, ..., iₖ}, this subset will contribute to f(T) for every T that contains this subset and is a superset of it.

For a specific subset C that sums to S, how many T contain C as a subset?
- T must contain all elements in C
- T can contain any subset of elements not in C

If there are N - k elements not in C, then there are 2^(N-k) possible T that contain C.

But we also need to ensure that T is non-empty, and for each such T containing C, we count C in f(T).

However, there's a constraint: we need to avoid double-counting. Each valid subset of elements that sums to S will be counted exactly once for each possible superset T that contains it.

Wait, let me reconsider. For each non-empty subset C of the original array that sums to S, it will contribute to f(T) for every non-empty subset T that contains C.

The number of such T is 2^(N - |C|) because we fix C to be in T, and we can freely choose any subset of the remaining N - |C| elements.

However, we need to be careful about the empty set. When we say "subset of T", we require non-empty subsets. But when C is non-empty (which it is, since we're only considering subsets that sum to S), then C itself is a non-empty subset of T.

So for each non-empty subset C (subset of indices) that has sum S, it contributes exactly 2^(N - |C|) to the overall sum.

Therefore, our answer is: sum over all non-empty subsets C of {1,2,...,N} such that sum of A_i for i in C equals S, of 2^(N - |C|).

## Algorithm

1. Use dynamic programming to count the number of ways to form subsets with sum S, and for each such subset, we need to track the size (number of elements).

2. Let dp[i][j][k] = number of ways to choose a subset from first i elements with sum j and size k.

3. But N, S ≤ 3000, so a 3D DP might be O(N×S×N) = O(3000×3000×3000) = O(2.7e10), which is too slow.

4. We can optimize: we only need sum over size k of 2^(N-k) × count(size=k, sum=S).

5. So we can do: dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j with exactly k elements.

6. Transition: for each element A[i], we can either include it or exclude it.

Actually, we can use a 2D DP where dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j using exactly k elements.

Initial state: dp[0][0] = 1 (empty subset)

For each element A[i]:
- For each possible sum j and size k, we can either skip A[i] (state remains) or include A[i] (state becomes j + A[i], k + 1)

We iterate through each element, updating the DP table.

Finally, for each size k from 1 to N, we add dp[S][k] × 2^(N-k) to our answer.

Wait, let me verify with the sample:
- N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]
- We need non-empty subsets that sum to 4.

Subsets that sum to 4:
- {3}: A[3]=4 → sum=4, size=1 → contributes 2^(3-1) = 4
- {1,2}: A[1]+A[2]=2+2=4 → sum=4, size=2 → contributes 2^(3-2) = 2

Total: 4 + 2 = 6 ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected output.

## Implementation

We'll use DP with dimensions [S+1][N+1], where dp[j][k] = number of ways to achieve sum j with exactly k elements.

We need to compute powers of 2 up to N (2^(N-k) for k from 1 to N).

```python
MOD = 998244353

def power2(n):
    return pow(2, n, MOD)

# Read input
N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# dp[j][k] = number of ways to have sum j using exactly k elements
# Initialize with dp[0][0] = 1
dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
dp[0][0] = 1

# Process each element
for a in A:
    # We need to iterate backwards to avoid using updated values in the same iteration
    new_dp = [row[:] for row in dp]  # Make a copy
    for j in range(S + 1):
        for k in range(N):
            if dp[j][k] > 0:
                new_sum = j + a
                if new_sum <= S:
                    new_k = k + 1
                    if new_k <= N:
                        new_dp[new_sum][new_k] = (new_dp[new_sum][new_k] + dp[j][k]) % MOD
    dp = new_dp

# Calculate result
result = 0
for k in range(1, N + 1):  # k from 1 to N (non-empty subsets)
    if dp[S][k] > 0:
        result = (result + dp[S][k] * power2(N - k)) % MOD

print(result)
```

- Add a=4 to (2,1): gives (6,2) - too big
- Add a=4 to (4,2): gives (8,3) - too big

So final dp[S][k] for S=4:
- dp[4][1] = 1 (from {3})
- dp[4][2] = 1 (from {1,2})

However, there's an issue with the DP update. When we create new_dp as a copy of dp, and then we update it from the original dp, we're overwriting. Actually, the logic is correct: we're considering each element and adding it to all existing states.

But we need to be careful about the order. Since we're processing each element one by one, and for each element we can choose to include or exclude it, the standard way is to iterate backwards in sum to avoid using updated values.

Actually, in our current approach, we create a new_dp from the current state, then update new_dp by adding the contribution of the current element to each existing state. This is equivalent to doing the "coin change" style DP where we process each denomination.

But wait, the standard knapsack with count of elements should be done by iterating backwards in sum. However, since we're not using the updated values in the same iteration (we're using the old dp values), our copy approach should be correct.

Let me verify the logic: we take the current state (before processing element a), and for each state (sum, size), we create a new state by adding element a (if sum + a ≤ S). This gives us the states that include the current element.

The total number of ways to form a subset including this element is correctly captured.

So the approach is correct.

```python
MOD = 998244353

def power2(n):
    return pow(2, n, MOD)

# Read input
N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# dp[j][k] = number of ways to have sum j using exactly k elements
dp = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(S + 1)]
dp[0][0] = 1

# Process each element
for a in A:
    # Create a new dp state based on current dp (this acts as "not taking a")
    # Then add the state from taking a
    new_dp = [row[:] for row in dp]  # Copy current state
    
    # For each possible sum and size in current state
    for j in range(S + 1):
        for k in range(N):
            if dp[j][k] > 0:
                new_sum = j + a
                if new_sum <= S:
                    new_k = k + 1
                    if new_k <= N:
                        new_dp[new_sum][new_k] = (new_dp[new_sum][new_k] + dp[j][k]) % MOD
    
    dp = new_dp

# Calculate result: for each size k, add dp[S][k] * 2^(N-k)
result = 0
for k in range(1, N + 1):  # k from 1 to N (non-empty subsets)
    result = (result + dp[S][k] * power2(N - k)) % MOD

print(result)
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the sum of f(T) over all non-empty subsets T of {1, 2, ..., N}, where f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets of T that sum to S.

Let me think about this differently. Instead of iterating over all possible subsets T and counting valid sub-subsets, I can reframe the problem:

For each possible subset of indices that sums to S, I need to count in how many larger subsets T this subset is contained.

In other words, if I have a subset of elements that sum to S, say with indices {i₁, i₂, ..., iₖ}, then this subset contributes 1 to f(T) for every T that contains all these indices and at least these indices (but T can contain other elements too).

So for a fixed subset of elements that sum to S, how many T contain this subset?

If a subset of indices has sum S, then any superset T that contains all these indices will have this subset as one of its valid sub-subsets.

The key insight is: **For each subset of the N elements that sums to S, the number of T that contain this subset is 2^(N - k) where k is the size of the subset** (since we can freely choose any subset of the remaining N-k elements to include in T).

Wait, that's not quite right. Actually, for a fixed subset of indices with sum S, any superset T that contains these indices will have this subset as a valid subset. The number of such T is 2^(N - k) where k is the size of the subset (since we can include or exclude each of the remaining N-k elements in T).

Therefore, the total answer is the sum over all non-empty subsets of indices that sum to S of 2^(N - k), where k is the size of that subset.

But wait - let me reconsider the original definition. f(T) counts the number of different non-empty subsets of T that sum to S. So if we have a subset of indices that sums to S, it contributes 1 to f(T) for every T that contains this subset.

So the total sum is: for each non-empty subset U of {1, 2, ..., N} such that sum of A_i for i in U equals S, add 2^(N - |U|).

This is because for a fixed U (a subset that sums to S), any T that contains U will have U as a valid subset (since U ⊆ T). The number of such T is 2^(N - |U|) (we can freely choose any subset of the elements not in U).

So our answer is: Σ_{all non-empty subsets U ⊆ {1,...,N}, sum(U) = S} 2^(N - |U|)

This can be rewritten as: 2^N × Σ_{all non-empty subsets U ⊆ {1,...,N}, sum(U) = S} 2^(-|U|)

Or more simply: Σ_{U: sum(U)=S} 2^(N - |U|)

Now I need to compute this sum efficiently.

I can use dynamic programming where dp[i][s] = sum of 2^(N - k) for all subsets of the first i elements that sum to s, where k is the size of the subset.

Wait, actually I need to track the sum of 2^(N - k) for each possible sum.

Since N ≤ 3000, and S ≤ 3000, I can use DP where:
- dp[s] = sum of (2^(N - k)) for all subsets that sum to s and have k elements

But I need to track the size of the subset. So I need a 2D DP: dp[i][s] = sum of 2^(N - k) for all subsets using first i elements that sum to s.

When I consider element i with value A_i, I can:
- Not include it: contribution remains
- Include it: then if I have a subset with sum s' and size k, the new subset has sum s' + A_i and size k+1, so contribution becomes 2^(N - (k+1)) = 2^(N - k - 1) = (1/2) × 2^(N - k)

So the recurrence is:
dp[i][s] = dp[i-1][s] + (if s >= A_i then (1/2) × dp[i-1][s - A_i] else 0)

But this involves fractions. Since we're working modulo 998244353, I need to work with modular inverses.

The contribution when including an element is multiplied by 1/2, which is 2^(-1) mod 998244353.

Let me define the DP state as:
dp[i][s] = sum of 2^(N - k) for all subsets of first i elements that sum to s.

Base case: dp[0][0] = 1 (empty subset), dp[0][s>0] = 0

Transition:
dp[i][s] = dp[i-1][s] + (if s >= A_i then dp[i-1][s - A_i] × inv(2) else 0)

After processing all N elements, the answer is the sum of dp[N][s] for all s = S.

But wait - this includes the empty subset. The empty subset has sum 0 and contributes 2^(N-0) = 2^N when it doesn't contribute to f(T) since we need non-empty subsets.

Actually, let me think again. In our DP, when we include a subset, we're summing 2^(N - k) for each subset. The empty subset contributes 2^(N - 0) = 2^N when it's in our sum, but the empty subset has sum 0, not S.

For our problem, we only care about subsets that sum to S, and we want the sum of 2^(N - k) for each such subset.

The empty subset has sum 0, so it won't contribute to our final answer when S > 0.

But what about S = 0? The problem states S ≥ 1, so we don't have to worry about it.

So the algorithm:
1. Initialize dp[0][0] = 1, and dp[0][s] = 0 for s > 0
2. For each element i from 1 to N:
   - For each sum s from 0 to S:
     - dp[i][s] = dp[i-1][s]  (don't take element i)
     - If s >= A_i, then dp[i][s] += dp[i-1][s - A_i] * inv_2
3. Answer = dp[N][S]

We need to compute modular inverse of 2 modulo 998244353.

Let me verify with the sample:
N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]

We want sum of 2^(3-k) for all non-empty subsets that sum to 4.

Possible subsets that sum to 4:
- {1,2}: indices 1,2, A₁=2, A₂=2, sum=4, size=2, contribution = 2^(3-2) = 2^1 = 2
- {3}: index 3, A₃=4, sum=4, size=1, contribution = 2^(3-1) = 2^2 = 4
- {1,3}: sum = 2+4=6, no
- {2,3}: sum = 2+4=6, no
- {1,2,3}: sum = 2+2+4=8, no

Wait, only {1,2} and {3} sum to 4.

So total contribution should be 2 + 4 = 6.

But according to my calculation above, for {1,2}: size=2, contributes 2^(3-2) = 2
For {3}: size=1, contributes 2^(3-1) = 4
Total = 6. Correct!

In my DP:
- Initially: dp[0][0] = 1
- After element 1 (A₁=2):
  - dp[1][0] = 1
  - dp[1][2] = 1 * inv(2) = 1/2 (mod 998244353)
- After element 2 (A₂=2):
  - dp[2][0] = 1
  - dp[2][2] = 1/2 (from not taking) + 1/2 (from taking element 2: dp[1][0] = 1 → 1 * 1/2 = 1/2)
  - dp[2][4] = 1/2 (from taking element 2: dp[1][2] = 1/2 → (1/2) * (1/2) = 1/4)
- After element 3 (A₃=4):
  - dp[3][0] = 1
  - dp[3][2] = dp[2][2] = 1/2 + 1/2 = 1
  - dp[3][4] = dp[2][4] + (dp[2][0] * 1/2) = 1/4 + (1 * 1/2) = 1/4 + 2/4 = 3/4

Wait, this gives 3/4, not 6. Something's wrong.

I see the issue. In my DP formulation, I'm not correctly handling the contribution.

Let me reconsider. When I say dp[i][s] = sum of 2^(N-k) for subsets of first i elements that sum to s, then when I add element i with value A_i:

- If I don't take it: contribution stays the same
- If I take it: the new subset has one more element, so the weight decreases by factor of 1/2 (since 2^(N-k-1) = (1/2) * 2^(N-k))

But in the transition, when I take element i from a subset with sum s-A_i and size k, the new size is k+1, so the contribution becomes 2^(N-k-1) = (1/2) * 2^(N-k).

So the recurrence should be:
dp[i][s] = dp[i-1][s] + (if s >= A_i then dp[i-1][s - A_i] * inv_2 else 0)

But with the sample:
N=3, S=4

dp[0][0] = 1

After element 1 (A₁=2):
dp[1][0] = 1
dp[1][2] = 0 + 1 * inv_2 = inv_2

After element 2 (A₂=2):
dp[2][0] = 1
dp[2][2] = dp[1][2] + dp[1][0] * inv_2 = inv_2 + 1 * inv_2 = 2 * inv_2 = 1
dp[2][4] = dp[1][4] + dp[1][2] * inv_2 = 0 + inv_2 * inv_2 = inv_2²

After element 3 (A₃=4):
dp[3][0] = 1
dp[3][2] = 1 + 0 = 1
dp[3][4] = dp[2][4] + dp[2][0] * inv_2 = inv_2² + 1 * inv_2 = inv_2 + inv_2²

This gives inv_2 + inv_2², which is 1/2 + 1/4 = 3/4.

But we need 6, not 3/4. 

I see the error! The problem is that I'm not using the right base. In my DP, I'm starting with empty set contribution 1, but when I take an element, I'm multiplying by inv_2, which is correct for the size increase.

But the actual contribution should be 2^(N-k), not the sum of 2^(N-k). In the example, for subset {1,2} (size 2): contribution should be 2^(3-2) = 2^1 = 2
For subset {3} (size 1): contribution should be 2^(3-1) = 4
Total = 6

In my DP:
- After processing all elements, dp[3][4] should be 6, not 3/4.

Where is the mistake in the DP?

I think I see it. The DP state is defined as sum of 2^(N-k) for subsets of first i elements that sum to s. But when I update with the new element, I'm using inv_2, which is correct.

Let me trace through manually with N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]:

Initial: dp[0][0] = 1, others 0

After element 1 (A=2):
- Don't take: sum 0 → contribution 1 (which is 2^(3-0) = 8? Wait no!)

This is my mistake! I need to track the exponent properly.

When I define dp[i][s] = sum of 2^(N-k) for subsets of first i elements that sum to s, then when I add an element, the exponent N-k changes from (N-k) to (N-k-1) = (N-k) - 1.

But N is fixed (3), so when I add an element, k increases by 1, so the power decreases by 1, so I multiply by 1/2.

But the initial value for the empty set should be 2^(N-0) = 2^N, not 1.

Ah! That's the error. I defined dp[0][0] = 1, but it should be 2^(N-0) = 2^N.

Let me fix this.

Define dp[i][s] = sum of 2^(N-k) for all subsets of first i elements that sum to s, where k is the size of the subset.

Base case: dp[0][0] = 2^N (empty subset has size 0, so contribution is 2^(N-0) = 2^N)

Then for element i with value A_i:
- Don't take it: contribution to dp[i][s] is dp[i-1][s]
- Take it: if s >= A_i, contribution is dp[i-1][s - A_i] * inv_2

Final answer: dp[N][S]

Let me verify with the sample:
N=3, so 2^N = 8
dp[0][0] = 8, others 0

After element 1 (A=2):
- dp[1][0] = 8
- dp[1][2] = 0 + 8 * inv_2 = 8/2 = 4

After element 2 (A=2):
- dp[2][0] = 8
- dp[2][2] = dp[1][2] + dp[1][0] * inv_2 = 4 + 8 * inv_2 = 4 + 4 = 8
- dp[2][4] = dp[1][4] + dp[1][2] * inv_2 = 0 + 4 * inv_2 = 4/2 = 2

After element 3 (A=4):
- dp[3][0] = 8
- dp[3][2] = dp[2][2] + dp[2][0] * inv_2 = 8 + 8 * inv_2 = 8 + 4 = 12
- dp[3][4] = dp[2][4] + dp[2][0] * inv_2 = 2 + 8 * inv_2 = 2 + 4 = 6

Yes! dp[3][4] = 6, which matches the expected answer.

Perfect! So the algorithm is:
1. Compute inv_2 = modular inverse of 2 mod 998244353
2. Compute base = 2^N mod 998244353
3. Initialize dp[0][0] = base, dp[0][s>0] = 0
4. For each element i from 0 to N-1:
   - For each sum s from 0 to S:
     - dp[i+1][s] = dp[i][s]  (don't take A[i])
     - If s >= A[i]: dp[i+1][s] += dp[i][s - A[i]] * inv_2
5. Answer = dp[N][S]

The time complexity is O(N*S), which is acceptable since N,S ≤ 3000.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    
    # Read input
    N, S = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Precompute modular inverse of 2
    inv_2 = pow(2, MOD - 2, MOD)
    
    # Precompute 2^N mod MOD
    base = pow(2, N, MOD)
    
    # DP array: dp[s] = sum of 2^(N-k) for subsets summing to s
    # We only need to keep track of the current state
    dp = [0] * (S + 1)
    dp[0] = base
    
    # Process each element
    for a in A:
        new_dp = [0] * (S + 1)
        for s in range(S + 1):
            # Don't take element a
            new_dp[s] = dp[s]
            # Take element a (if possible)
            if s >= a:
                new_dp[s] = (new_dp[s] + dp[s - a] * inv_2) % MOD
        dp = new_dp
    
    print(dp[S])

solve()
```

Given are a sequence of N positive integers A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N and another positive integer S.

For a non-empty subset T of the set \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}, let us define f(T) as follows:

 - f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets \{x_1, x_2, \ldots , x_k \} of T such that A_{x_1}+A_{x_2}+\cdots +A_{x_k} = S.
Find the sum of f(T) over all 2^N-1 subsets T of \{1, 2, \ldots , N \}. Since the sum can be enormous, print it modulo 998244353.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq S \leq 3000
 - 1 \leq A_i \leq 3000

N S
A_1 A_2 ... A_N

-----Output-----
Print the sum of f(T) modulo 998244353.

-----Sample Input-----
3 4
2 2 4

-----Sample Output-----
6

 - f(\{1\}) = 0
 - f(\{2\}) = 0
 - f(\{3\}) = 1 (One subset \{3\} satisfies the condition.)
 - f(\{1, 2\}) = 1 (\{1, 2\})
 - f(\{2, 3\}) = 1 (\{3\})
 - f(\{1, 3\}) = 1 (\{3\})
 - f(\{1, 2, 3\}) = 2 (\{1, 2\}, \{3\})

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the sum of f(T) over all non-empty subsets T of {1, 2, ..., N}, where f(T) is the number of different non-empty subsets of T that sum to S.

In other words, for each possible subset T of our original array, we count how many of its non-empty subsets sum to S, then sum all these counts across all possible T.

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating over all subsets T and then over all subsets of T, we can change our perspective. Let's think about it differently:

For each possible subset of the original array that sums to S, how many times does it contribute to the final answer?

A subset {i₁, i₂, ..., iₖ} that sums to S will be counted in f(T) for every subset T that contains this subset as a subpart.

In other words, if we have a subset B of indices that sums to S, then B will contribute to f(T) for every T that contains B as a subset. 

For a fixed subset B (with sum S), how many T's contain B? 
- We need to choose any subset of the remaining (N - |B|) elements to be in T
- Each of these remaining elements can either be included or excluded from T
- So there are 2^(N-|B|) such T's

But wait - we need to be careful. The subset B itself must be a subset of T. For each such T containing B, the subset B is counted in f(T) because it's a non-empty subset of T that sums to S.

Therefore, the contribution of subset B to the final answer is 2^(N-|B|).

So the total answer is the sum over all non-empty subsets B of the original array that have sum S of 2^(N-|B|).

## Algorithm

1. For each possible subset of the array that sums to S, calculate 2^(N-|B|)
2. Sum all these contributions

However, checking all 2^N subsets is too expensive. We need a dynamic programming approach.

Let's use DP where dp[i][s] = list of all possible subset sizes for subsets of first i elements that sum to s.

But we need the sum of 2^(N-|B|) over all subsets B that sum to S.

We can reframe: for each sum s, we want to track the sum of 2^(N-|size|) for all subsets that achieve sum s.

Let dp[i][s] = sum over all subsets of first i elements that sum to s of 2^(N-|subset|)

But since we're multiplying by 2^(N-|size|), and 2^(N-|size|) = 2^N / 2^|size|, we can think of it as:

dp[i][s] = sum over all subsets of first i elements that sum to s of 2^(N-|subset|)

We can compute this with DP: 
- Initially, dp[0][0] = 1 (empty subset of sum 0 with size 0)
- For each element A[i], we update dp[i+1][s] from dp[i][s] and dp[i][s-A[i]]

But when we add element A[i], if we include it in a subset, the size increases by 1, so we multiply by 2^(-1) = 1/2.

Since we're working with integers modulo 998244353, we need to work with modular inverses.

Actually, let me reconsider. Let dp[s] = sum over all subsets that sum to s of 2^(N-|subset|)

When we add a new element A[i], for each subset with sum s, we can either:
1. Not include A[i]: contribution remains
2. Include A[i]: the size increases by 1, so 2^(N-|subset|-1) = 2^(N-1-|subset|) = (1/2) * 2^(N-|subset|)

So the recurrence is:
dp_new[s] = dp_old[s] + (1/2) * dp_old[s-A[i]]

With initial dp[0] = 2^N (empty subset has size 0, so 2^(N-0) = 2^N)

Wait, this is problematic because we want to exclude the empty subset in the end, but we're including empty subsets in our DP.

Let me redefine: we want to count subsets that sum to S, not including the empty subset.

Actually, let's go back to the contribution idea:
- Each non-empty subset B that sums to S contributes 2^(N-|B|) to the final answer
- We need to sum this over all such B

We can use DP where dp[s] represents the sum of 2^(N-|B|) over all subsets B (including empty) that sum to s.

Then our answer is dp[S] minus the contribution of the empty subset (if S=0).

But S ≥ 1, so there's no empty subset summing to S. So dp[S] is exactly our answer.

Actually, the empty subset only sums to 0, so if S > 0, we don't have to worry about it.

So let's set:
- dp[s] = sum of 2^(N-|B|) for all subsets B (including empty) that sum to s

We initialize dp[0] = 2^N (empty subset has size 0, so 2^(N-0) = 2^N)

For each element A[i], we update:
new_dp[s] = dp[s] + (1/2) * dp[s - A[i]]

But this is for each s, and we need to be careful about the recurrence.

Actually, the recurrence should be:
new_dp[s] = dp[s] + (1/2) * dp[s - A[i]]  (for s >= A[i])

But when we include A[i], every subset that sums to s-A[i] gets a new subset that sums to s with size increased by 1, so its contribution becomes (1/2) * original contribution.

So the recurrence is correct.

We need to compute modular inverses since we're dividing by 2.

In modulo 998244353, the inverse of 2 is (998244353+1)//2 = 499122177.

## Steps:

1. Read input
2. Initialize dp[0] = 2^N mod 998244353, dp[s] = 0 for s > 0

   - For each s from S down to A[i]:

For each element A[i], we update from high to low to avoid using updated values.

```python
inv2 = pow(2, MOD-2, MOD)  # inverse of 2 modulo MOD
```

Let me verify with the sample:
N=3, S=4, A=[2,2,4]

Initial: dp[0] = 2^3 = 8, all others 0

After first element 2:
- s=2: dp[2] = 0 + (1/2)*dp[0] = (1/2)*8 = 4
- Other values unchanged
- dp = {0:8, 2:4}

After second element 2:
- s=2: dp[2] = 4 + (1/2)*dp[0] = 4 + 4 = 8
- s=4: dp[4] = 0 + (1/2)*dp[2] = (1/2)*4 = 2
- dp = {0:8, 2:8, 4:2}

After third element 4:
- s=4: dp[4] = 2 + (1/2)*dp[0] = 2 + 4 = 6
- s=6: dp[6] = 0 + (1/2)*dp[2] = (1/2)*8 = 4
- s=8: dp[8] = 0 + (1/2)*dp[4] = (1/2)*2 = 1
- dp = {0:8, 2:8, 4:6, 6:4, 8:1}

So answer = dp[4] = 6, which matches!

## Implementation:

```python
MOD = 998244353

# Read input
N, S = map(int, input().split())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate 2^N mod MOD
power_of_2 = pow(2, N, MOD)

# dp[s] represents sum of 2^(N-|B|) for all subsets that sum to s
dp = [0] * (S + 1)
dp[0] = power_of_2  # empty subset has size 0, so 2^(N-0) = 2^N

# Process each element
inv2 = pow(2, MOD-2, MOD)  # modular inverse of 2

for a in A:
    # Update dp in reverse order to avoid using updated values
    new_dp = dp[:]  # copy current state
    for s in range(S, a-1, -1):
        new_dp[s] = (new_dp[s] + inv2 * dp[s - a]) % MOD
    dp = new_dp

print(dp[S])
```

Let me trace again with the sample to be safe.

- s=2: new_dp[2] = 0 + (1/2)*dp[0] = 4
- new_dp = [8, 0, 4, 0, 0]
- After processing, dp becomes [8,0,4,0,0]

- So only s=4 is updated: new_dp[4] = 6
- dp = [8,0,8,0,6]

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The busses in Berland are equipped with a video surveillance system. The system records information about changes in the number of passengers in a bus after stops.

If x is the number of passengers in a bus just before the current bus stop and y is the number of passengers in the bus just after current bus stop, the system records the number y-x. So the system records show how number of passengers changed.

The test run was made for single bus and n bus stops. Thus, the system recorded the sequence of integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_n (exactly one number for each bus stop), where a_i is the record for the bus stop i. The bus stops are numbered from 1 to n in chronological order.

Determine the number of possible ways how many people could be in the bus before the first bus stop, if the bus has a capacity equals to w (that is, at any time in the bus there should be from 0 to w passengers inclusive).

Input

The first line contains two integers n and w (1 ≤ n ≤ 1 000, 1 ≤ w ≤ 10^{9}) — the number of bus stops and the capacity of the bus.

The second line contains a sequence a_1, a_2, ..., a_n (-10^{6} ≤ a_i ≤ 10^{6}), where a_i equals to the number, which has been recorded by the video system after the i-th bus stop.

Output

Print the number of possible ways how many people could be in the bus before the first bus stop, if the bus has a capacity equals to w. If the situation is contradictory (i.e. for any initial number of passengers there will be a contradiction), print 0.

Examples

Input

3 5
2 1 -3

-1 1


Output

4

4 10
2 4 1 2

2

In the second example initially in the bus could be 1, 2, 3 or 4 passengers.

In the third example initially in the bus could be 0 or 1 passenger.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a bus with capacity `w` (maximum passengers allowed)
2. We have `n` bus stops with recorded changes in passenger count: `a_1, a_2, ..., a_n`
3. We need to find how many possible initial passenger counts (before stop 1) are valid
4. At any point, the number of passengers must be between 0 and `w` (inclusive)

## Key Insights

Let's say the initial number of passengers is `x`. After each stop, the passenger count changes as follows:
- After stop 1: `x + a_1`
- After stop 2: `x + a_1 + a_2`
- After stop i: `x + sum(a_1 to a_i)`

Let `s_i = sum(a_1 to a_i)` be the cumulative sum after stop i.

Then after stop i, the passenger count is `x + s_i`.

For the situation to be valid:
- `x + s_i ≥ 0` for all i (can't have negative passengers)
- `x + s_i ≤ w` for all i (can't exceed capacity)

This gives us two constraints:
1. `x ≥ -s_i` for all i (so that `x + s_i ≥ 0`)
2. `x ≤ w - s_i` for all i (so that `x + s_i ≤ w`)

## Solution Steps

1. Calculate the cumulative sum `s_i` after each stop
2. For each cumulative sum `s_i`, we get constraints on `x`:
   - From `x + s_i ≥ 0`: `x ≥ -s_i`
   - From `x + s_i ≤ w`: `x ≤ w - s_i`
3. Find the intersection of all these constraints
4. The valid range for `x` is `[lower_bound, upper_bound]` where:
   - `lower_bound = max(-s_i)` over all i (we need to satisfy all x ≥ -s_i)
   - `upper_bound = min(w - s_i)` over all i (we need to satisfy all x ≤ w - s_i)
5. If `lower_bound > upper_bound`, then no valid solution exists (return 0)
6. Otherwise, the number of integer solutions is `upper_bound - lower_bound + 1`

## Edge Cases

- If any constraint makes the range invalid (lower_bound > upper_bound), return 0
- The initial count must be an integer (which it will be since we're dealing with integers)

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: n=3, w=5, a=[2,1,-3]
- s_1 = 2, s_2 = 3, s_3 = 0
- Constraints:
  - x ≥ -2 (from x + 2 ≥ 0)
  - x ≥ -3 (from x + 3 ≥ 0) 
  - x ≥ 0 (from x + 0 ≥ 0)
  - x ≤ 5-2 = 3 (from x + 2 ≤ 5)
  - x ≤ 5-3 = 2 (from x + 3 ≤ 5)
  - x ≤ 5-0 = 5 (from x + 0 ≤ 5)
- So x ≥ max(-2,-3,0) = 0
- x ≤ min(3,2,5) = 2
- Answer: 2 - 0 + 1 = 3 ✓

**Example 2**: n=2, w=4, a=[-1,1]
- s_1 = -1, s_2 = 0
- Constraints:
  - x ≥ 1 (from x + (-1) ≥ 0)
  - x ≥ 0 (from x + 0 ≥ 0)
  - x ≤ 4 - (-1) = 5 (from x + (-1) ≤ 4)
  - x ≤ 4 - 0 = 4 (from x + 0 ≤ 4)
- So x ≥ max(1,0) = 1
- x ≤ min(5,4) = 4
- Answer: 4 - 1 + 1 = 4 ✓

```python
n, w = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate cumulative sums
cumulative_sums = [0]  # before first stop, it's 0
current_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    current_sum += a[i]
    cumulative_sums.append(current_sum)

# Find the constraints on initial passengers x
lower_bound = 0  # x >= 0 (can't have negative passengers)
upper_bound = w  # x <= w (initially at most w passengers)

# For each cumulative sum after stop i, we get constraints
for i in range(1, n + 1):  # i from 1 to n (after each stop)
    s_i = cumulative_sums[i]
    # x + s_i >= 0  => x >= -s_i
    lower_bound = max(lower_bound, -s_i)
    # x + s_i <= w  => x <= w - s_i
    upper_bound = min(upper_bound, w - s_i)

# If the range is invalid, return 0
if lower_bound > upper_bound:
    print(0)
else:
    print(upper_bound - lower_bound + 1)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a bus with capacity `w` (maximum passengers is `w`)
2. We have `n` bus stops with recordings `a_1, a_2, ..., a_n` where `a_i = y - x` (passengers after stop i minus passengers before stop i)
3. We need to find how many possible initial passenger counts (before first stop) are valid
4. At any point, the number of passengers must be between 0 and w (inclusive)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand the relationship
Let `p_0` be the initial number of passengers before stop 1.
After stop 1: `p_1 = p_0 + a_1`
After stop 2: `p_2 = p_0 + a_1 + a_2`
After stop i: `p_i = p_0 + sum(a_1 to a_i)`

### Step 2: Constraints
For each stop i, we need `0 ≤ p_i ≤ w`
So: `0 ≤ p_0 + sum(a_1 to a_i) ≤ w` for all i from 1 to n

### Step 3: Find the range of p_0
Let `s_i = sum(a_1 to a_i)` (cumulative sum up to stop i)

For each i, we have:
- `p_0 + s_i ≥ 0` → `p_0 ≥ -s_i`
- `p_0 + s_i ≤ w` → `p_0 ≤ w - s_i`

So for each i, `p_0` must be in the range `[-s_i, w - s_i]`

### Step 4: Overall range
The valid values of `p_0` are the intersection of all these ranges for i = 1 to n.

The overall lower bound: `L = max(-s_i for all i)`
The overall upper bound: `U = min(w - s_i for all i)`

If `L > U`, then no valid initial count exists → return 0
Otherwise, the number of valid initial counts is `U - L + 1`

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- If the intersection is empty, return 0
- Initial count must be a non-negative integer (but since p_0 ≥ 0 is already handled by our constraints)

Let me trace through the first example:
- n=3, w=5, a=[2,1,-3]
- s_1 = 2, so p_0 ≥ -2 and p_0 ≤ 5-2 = 3
- s_2 = 2+1 = 3, so p_0 ≥ -3 and p_0 ≤ 5-3 = 2  
- s_3 = 2+1-3 = 0, so p_0 ≥ 0 and p_0 ≤ 5-0 = 5
- L = max(-2, -3, 0) = 0
- U = min(3, 2, 5) = 2
- Valid values: 0, 1, 2 → count = 3 ✓

Second example:
- n=2, w=4, a=[-1,1]
- s_1 = -1, so p_0 ≥ 1 and p_0 ≤ 4-(-1) = 5
- s_2 = -1+1 = 0, so p_0 ≥ 0 and p_0 ≤ 4-0 = 4
- L = max(1, 0) = 1
- U = min(5, 4) = 4
- Valid values: 1,2,3,4 → count = 4 ✓

## Implementation

```python
n, w = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate cumulative sums
cumulative_sums = [0] * n
cumulative_sums[0] = a[0]
for i in range(1, n):
    cumulative_sums[i] = cumulative_sums[i-1] + a[i]

# Find the bounds for initial passengers
lower_bound = 0  # We need non-negative initial passengers
upper_bound = w

# For each stop, update the bounds
for i in range(n):
    # At stop i+1, passengers = initial + cumulative_sum[i]
    # So: 0 <= initial + cumulative_sum[i] <= w
    # This gives: -cumulative_sum[i] <= initial <= w - cumulative_sum[i]
    lower_bound = max(lower_bound, -cumulative_sums[i])
    upper_bound = min(upper_bound, w - cumulative_sums[i])

# If the bounds are valid (lower <= upper), return the count
if lower_bound > upper_bound:
    print(0)
else:
    print(upper_bound - lower_bound + 1)
```

Wait, I made a mistake in initialization. The initial passengers `p_0` can be any non-negative value, but our constraints ensure it's within bounds. Let me revise:

Actually, we don't need to start with `lower_bound = 0` because the constraint `p_0 ≥ -s_i` for each i already covers the non-negativity when we take the max.

The correct approach is to find the intersection of all constraints:

```python
n, w = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate cumulative sums up to each stop
cumulative_sums = []
current_sum = 0
for i in range(n):
    current_sum += a[i]
    cumulative_sums.append(current_sum)

# Find the range for initial passengers p0
# For each cumulative sum s_i, we need 0 <= p0 + s_i <= w
# So p0 >= -s_i and p0 <= w - s_i
min_initial = 0
max_initial = w

for s in cumulative_sums:
    min_initial = max(min_initial, -s)
    max_initial = min(max_initial, w - s)

if min_initial > max_initial:
    print(0)
else:
    print(max_initial - min_initial + 1)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a bus with capacity `w` (0 to w passengers at any time)
2. We have `n` bus stops with recorded changes `a_1, a_2, ..., a_n`
3. Each `a_i` represents the change in passengers from before stop i to after stop i
4. We need to find how many possible initial passenger counts (before first stop) are valid

## Key Insights

1. If we let `x` be the initial number of passengers before stop 1, then after stop 1 the number of passengers is `x + a_1`
2. After stop 2: `x + a_1 + a_2`
3. After stop i: `x + sum(a_1 to a_i)`

4. At any point, the passenger count must be between 0 and w (inclusive)

5. So for each stop i, we have the constraint:
   - `0 ≤ x + prefix_sum[i] ≤ w`
   where `prefix_sum[i] = a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i`

6. For each prefix sum, this gives us constraints on x:
   - `x ≥ -prefix_sum[i]`
   - `x ≤ w - prefix_sum[i]`

7. The valid x values must satisfy ALL these constraints simultaneously.

## Algorithm

1. Calculate prefix sums: `prefix_sum[i] = a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i`
2. For each prefix sum, derive the constraints on x:
   - Lower bound: `x ≥ -prefix_sum[i]`
   - Upper bound: `x ≤ w - prefix_sum[i]`
3. Combine all constraints to find the valid range for x
4. The valid range for x is `[max(lower_bounds), min(upper_bounds)]`
5. If this range is valid (lower ≤ upper), then the number of possible values is `max(0, min(upper_bounds) - max(lower_bounds) + 1)`
6. If the range is invalid (lower > upper), return 0

## Step-by-step Example 1:
- n=3, w=5, a=[2,1,-3]
- prefix sums: [2, 3, 0]
- Constraints:
  - After stop 1: 0 ≤ x+2 ≤ 5 → -2 ≤ x ≤ 3
  - After stop 2: 0 ≤ x+3 ≤ 5 → -3 ≤ x ≤ 2  
  - After stop 3: 0 ≤ x+0 ≤ 5 → 0 ≤ x ≤ 5
- Combined: max(-2,-3,0) = 0, min(3,2,5) = 2
- Valid x: from 0 to 2 → 3 values (0,1,2)

## Implementation

```python
n, w = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate prefix sums
prefix_sums = [0] * n
prefix_sums[0] = a[0]
for i in range(1, n):
    prefix_sums[i] = prefix_sums[i-1] + a[i]

# Find the overall constraints on initial passengers x
min_initial = 0  # x must be at least 0
max_initial = w  # x must be at most w

# For each prefix sum, add constraints
for i in range(n):
    # After stop i+1, passengers = x + prefix_sums[i]
    # We need 0 <= x + prefix_sums[i] <= w
    # So: -prefix_sums[i] <= x <= w - prefix_sums[i]
    
    lower_bound = -prefix_sums[i]
    upper_bound = w - prefix_sums[i]
    
    # Update the global bounds
    min_initial = max(min_initial, lower_bound)
    max_initial = min(max_initial, upper_bound)

# If the valid range is invalid, return 0
if min_initial > max_initial:
    print(0)
else:
    # Number of possible initial values
    print(max_initial - min_initial + 1)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The busses in Berland are equipped with a video surveillance system. The system records information about changes in the number of passengers in a bus after stops.

If x is the number of passengers in a bus just before the current bus stop and y is the number of passengers in the bus just after current bus stop, the system records the number y-x. So the system records show how number of passengers changed.

The test run was made for single bus and n bus stops. Thus, the system recorded the sequence of integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_n (exactly one number for each bus stop), where a_i is the record for the bus stop i. The bus stops are numbered from 1 to n in chronological order.

Determine the number of possible ways how many people could be in the bus before the first bus stop, if the bus has a capacity equals to w (that is, at any time in the bus there should be from 0 to w passengers inclusive).

Input

The first line contains two integers n and w (1 ≤ n ≤ 1 000, 1 ≤ w ≤ 10^{9}) — the number of bus stops and the capacity of the bus.

The second line contains a sequence a_1, a_2, ..., a_n (-10^{6} ≤ a_i ≤ 10^{6}), where a_i equals to the number, which has been recorded by the video system after the i-th bus stop.

Output

Print the number of possible ways how many people could be in the bus before the first bus stop, if the bus has a capacity equals to w. If the situation is contradictory (i.e. for any initial number of passengers there will be a contradiction), print 0.

Examples

Input

3 5
2 1 -3


Output

3


Input

2 4
-1 1


Output

4


Input

4 10
2 4 1 2


Output

2

Note

In the first example initially in the bus could be 0, 1 or 2 passengers.

In the second example initially in the bus could be 1, 2, 3 or 4 passengers.

In the third example initially in the bus could be 0 or 1 passenger.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs).
