Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Kevin has just recevied his disappointing results on the USA Identification of Cows Olympiad (USAICO) in the form of a binary string of length n. Each character of Kevin's string represents Kevin's score on one of the n questions of the olympiad—'1' for a correctly identified cow and '0' otherwise.

However, all is not lost. Kevin is a big proponent of alternative thinking and believes that his score, instead of being the sum of his points, should be the length of the longest alternating subsequence of his string. Here, we define an alternating subsequence of a string as a not-necessarily contiguous subsequence where no two consecutive elements are equal. For example, {0, 1, 0, 1}, {1, 0, 1}, and {1, 0, 1, 0} are alternating sequences, while {1, 0, 0} and {0, 1, 0, 1, 1} are not.

Kevin, being the sneaky little puffball that he is, is willing to hack into the USAICO databases to improve his score. In order to be subtle, he decides that he will flip exactly one substring—that is, take a contiguous non-empty substring of his score and change all '0's in that substring to '1's and vice versa. After such an operation, Kevin wants to know the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that his string could have.

Input

The first line contains the number of questions on the olympiad n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000).

The following line contains a binary string of length n representing Kevin's results on the USAICO. 

Output

Output a single integer, the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that Kevin can create in his string after flipping a single substring.

Examples

Input

8
10000011


Output

5


Input

2
01


Output

2

Note

In the first sample, Kevin can flip the bolded substring '10000011' and turn his string into '10011011', which has an alternating subsequence of length 5: '10011011'.

In the second sample, Kevin can flip the entire string and still have the same score.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string of length n
2. We can flip exactly one contiguous substring (change 0s to 1s and 1s to 0s)
3. We want to maximize the length of the longest alternating subsequence
4. An alternating subsequence has no two consecutive equal elements

## Key Insights

1. In any binary string, the longest alternating subsequence is essentially the number of "transitions" between 0s and 1s plus 1.

2. For example:
   - "0101" has transitions at positions 0→1, 1→0, 0→1 → length = 4
   - "0011" has transitions at positions 1→2 → length = 3
   - "01" has transition at position 0→1 → length = 2

3. So the alternating subsequence length = 1 + number of adjacent pairs (i, i+1) where s[i] ≠ s[i+1]

4. When we flip a contiguous substring, we're essentially changing the pattern of alternations in that region.

5. The key insight is that flipping a substring can only change the alternating subsequence length by at most 2 (or sometimes by 0, or even decrease).

## Strategy

Let me think about what happens when we flip a substring:

1. In a binary string, the alternating subsequence length is equal to 1 + number of positions where adjacent elements differ.

2. When we flip a contiguous substring, the changes occur at the boundaries of the flipped region and within the flipped region.

3. Specifically:
   - At the left boundary (where flipped part meets non-flipped part), we might create or destroy a transition
   - At the right boundary (where flipped part meets non-flipped part), we might create or destroy a transition  
   - Inside the flipped part, the internal transitions might change

4. However, there's a key observation: flipping a substring doesn't create new transitions in a simple way. 

Let me reconsider with examples:

For "10000011":
- Current alternating subsequence: "10" at start, then "0"→"0" (no transition), "0"→"0" (no transition), "0"→"0" (no transition), "0"→"1" (transition), "1"→"1" (no transition)
- Transitions at positions: 0→1 (1→0), 5→6 (0→1)
- So length = 1 + 2 = 3? Wait, that seems wrong.

Actually, let me recalculate "10000011":
- Position 0: '1', position 1: '0' → transition
- Position 1: '0', position 2: '0' → no transition
- Position 2: '0', position 3: '0' → no transition
- Position 3: '0', position 4: '0' → no transition
- Position 4: '0', position 5: '0' → no transition
- Position 5: '0', position 6: '1' → transition
- Position 6: '1', position 7: '1' → no transition

So transitions at indices 0 and 5 → total length = 1 + 2 = 3? But expected output is 5.

Wait, I'm misunderstanding alternating subsequences. 

The problem says "longest alternating subsequence" - this means we can pick non-consecutive elements that alternate.

For "1100", the longest alternating subsequence could be "10" or "10" → length 2.

But for "1001", we could pick positions 0,2: "10" → length 2, or positions 0,3: "11" → not alternating, or positions 0,1,3: "101" → alternating, length 3.

Actually, in "1001", we can pick 0,1,3: "101" → this is alternating (1→0→1), length 3.

How do we compute the maximum length?

**Key insight**: In any binary string, the maximum length of an alternating subsequence is equal to the number of "runs" (maximal contiguous groups of identical characters) in the string.

For example:
- "10000011": runs are "1", "00000", "11" → 3 runs → max alternating subsequence length = 3
- "1010": runs are "1", "0", "1", "0" → 4 runs → length 4

Wait, that gives 3 for "10000011", but expected is 5.

No, that can't be right. Let me think again.

Actually, in an alternating subsequence, we can pick one element from each run and they will automatically alternate. So the maximum alternating subsequence length is indeed equal to the number of runs.

For "10000011": 
- Run 1: "1" (length 1) 
- Run 2: "00000" (length 5)
- Run 3: "11" (length 2)
- Total runs = 3

But expected output is 5, not 3.

I think I'm missing something fundamental.

Let me check the example: "10000011" → flip substring to get "10011011" → has alternating subsequence of length 5.

In "10011011": 
- Positions: 1,0,0,1,1,0,1,1
- Let me find alternating subsequence: 1,0,1,0,1 → that's positions 0,1,3,5,6 → values: 1,0,1,0,1 → alternating, length 5.

So the length is 5.

But how many runs? In "10011011":
- Run 1: "1" (pos 0)
- Run 2: "00" (pos 1-2)
- Run 3: "11" (pos 3-4)
- Run 4: "0" (pos 5)
- Run 5: "11" (pos 6-7)
- That's 5 runs.

Aha! So the maximum alternating subsequence length in a binary string is exactly equal to the number of runs (contiguous groups of identical characters).

So the problem reduces to: we can flip exactly one contiguous substring, and we want to maximize the number of runs in the resulting string.

## Algorithm

1. Count the current number of runs in the string
2. Try flipping every possible contiguous substring and see which one maximizes the number of runs
3. But n can be up to 100,000, so we can't try all O(n²) substrings

We need to find a pattern.

When we flip a substring, what happens to the number of runs?

Let's say we have positions i to j flipped.

The changes occur at:
- Left boundary: between position i-1 and i
- Right boundary: between position j and j+1
- Inside the flipped region: all transitions are inverted, and also boundaries are affected

But there's a key insight: the number of runs in a binary string is equal to 1 + number of adjacent pairs where s[i] ≠ s[i+1].

So if we can compute how the number of transitions changes when we flip a substring, we can find the maximum.

Let me define T = total number of transitions (adjacent pairs where s[i] ≠ s[i+1])

Then number of runs = T + 1

So maximizing runs is equivalent to maximizing T.

When we flip substring [l, r], what happens to T?

The transitions that change are:
1. At position l-1 and l (if l > 0)
2. At position r and r+1 (if r < n-1)
3. Inside [l, r]: every adjacent pair within the flipped region has its transition status flipped

Let's say we have a transition at position k (between s[k] and s[k+1]). If we flip a substring containing k and k+1, then the transition flips.

Specifically:
- If both k and k+1 are outside [l,r]: transition unchanged
- If exactly one of k or k+1 is in [l,r]: transition might change depending on position
- If both k and k+1 are in [l,r]: transition flips

Actually, a transition at position k (between k and k+1) changes if and only if the flip affects both positions k and k+1, or only one of them, but the values change.

After flipping, if s[k] and s[k+1] are both flipped, then their difference flips.
If exactly one is flipped, then the difference flips.
If neither is flipped, difference unchanged.

So actually, a transition at position k flips if and only if k is in [l, r] or k+1 is in [l, r]? 

Wait: the transition at position k depends on s[k] and s[k+1].

What is f(l,r)?

For a fixed k, (k in [l,r]) XOR (k+1 in [l,r]).

k in [l,r] means l ≤ k ≤ r
k+1 in [l,r] means l ≤ k+1 ≤ r, i.e., l ≤ k+1 ≤ r

The condition is (k in [l,r]) XOR (k+1 in [l,r])

This is true when:
- k in [l,r] and k+1 not in [l,r] → k ≤ r and k+1 > r → k+1 > r → k ≥ r
- k not in [l,r] and k+1 in [l,r] → k < l and l ≤ k+1 ≤ r → k+1 ≥ l and k+1 ≤ r and k < l → k+1 ≥ l and k < l and k+1 ≤ r → k+1 ≥ l and k < l and k+1 ≤ r

So cases:
1. k ≤ r and k+1 > r → k > r-1 → k ≥ r (since k integer)
   - But k ≤ r and k ≥ r → k = r
   - So k = r is a case when k in [l,r] and k+1 not in [l,r]
2. k < l and k+1 in [l,r] → k+1 ≥ l and k+1 ≤ r and k < l → k < l and k+1 ≥ l → k ≥ l-1
   - So l-1 ≤ k < l

Therefore, f(l,r) = number of k such that either:
- k = r (and r ≥ l), or
- l-1 ≤ k < l

But k = r is valid only if l ≤ r (which is true since l ≤ r)
k = r satisfies k in [l,r] and k+1 not in [l,r] if k+1 > r, i.e., r+1 > r, always true, and k=r ≤ r, so always.

For the second case: k such that l-1 ≤ k < l

This means k can be from max(0, l-1) to min(l-1, n-2)

The values of k are from l-1 down to 0, but only if l-1 ≥ 0.

So the count is:
- From the first case: always 1 (k=r)
- From the second case: the number of integers k such that max(0, l-1) ≤ k < l

Since we want k < l and k ≥ l-1, the only integers are k = l-1.

The error is in the formula for f(l,r).

Let me recompute f(l,r) for "01" with l=1, r=1.

After flipping [1,1]: original s[1]=1, becomes 0, so string is "00" → no transition → T=0, original T=1, so change = -1.

But according to my earlier derivation, f(l,r) should be the number of k where exactly one of k or k+1 is in [l,r].

For l=1,r=1, n=2:
- k ranges from 0 to 0 (n-2=0)
- k=0: k=0 not in [1,1], k+1=1 in [1,1] → exactly one → count=1
- So f(1,1)=1

But the transition at k=0: between s[0] and s[1]
- Original: s[0]=0, s[1]=1 → difference true
- After flip: s[0]=0, s[1]=0 → difference false
- So transition changes from true to false → one change, so change in T is -1, not +1.

I see the mistake! When the transition changes, if it was true, it becomes false → T decreases by 1.

In my earlier analysis, I said the transition at k flips if exactly one of k or k+1 is in [l,r], and I said that means the difference flips, so T changes by +1.

But in this case, the difference changes from true to false, so T changes by -1.

So the net change in T is not +f(l,r), but it is the change in the number of transitions.

When a transition flips from true to false, T decreases by 1.
When it flips from false to true, T increases by 1.

In the case where exactly one of k or k+1 is in [l,r], the transition flips, but whether it increases or decreases depends on the original value.

Therefore, we cannot simply say the change is +f(l,r).

This is a much more complex problem.

Perhaps there's a simpler approach.

After research and thinking, I recall that in any binary string, the longest alternating subsequence is simply the number of runs.

And when we flip a substring, the number of runs can change in certain ways.

But perhaps there's a known result or a simpler observation.

Another observation: the maximum number of runs in a binary string is n (alternating like 010101...).

In any string, the number of runs is at least 1 and at most n.

We start with some initial number of runs.

When we flip a substring, how does the number of runs change?

The runs change at the boundaries of the flipped region.

Specific-ally, the runs that are affected are:
- The run that ends at position l-1 (if l>0)
- The run that starts at position l
- The run that ends at position r (if r < n-1)
- The run that starts at position r+1 (if r < n-1)

And within the flipped region, the runs might merge or split.

But this is messy.

After more thought, I found a known problem: the maximum number of runs after one flip.

Upon reflection, I believe the maximum possible number of runs after one flip is either the original number of runs, or we can increase it by at most 2.

In the first example, it goes from 3 runs to 5 runs, increase of 2.

In the second example, it goes from 2 runs to 2 runs, no increase.

So is it always possible to increase by 2 when n≥3?

For a string with only one run (all 0s or all 1s), after flipping any substring, we might get a string with 2 or 3 runs.

For example, "000" (n=3), runs=1
- Flip [0,0]: "100" → runs: "1", "00" → 2 runs
- Flip [0,1]: "110" → "11", "0" → 2 runs
- Flip [1,2]: "011" → "0", "11" → 2 runs
- Flip [0,2]: "111" → 1 run
So maximum is 2 runs, so increase of 1.

Can we get 3 runs? "010" has 3 runs. Can we get that from "000" with one flip? 
- Flip [0,1]: "110" → "11", "0" → 2 runs
- Flip [0,2]: "111" → 1 run
- Flip [1,1]: "010" → s[1] from 0 to 1, so positions: 0:0, 1:1, 2:0 → "010" → runs: "0", "1", "0" → 3 runs! Yes.

So from "000" (1 run) to "010" (3 runs) with one flip of position 1.

So increase by 2.

So in general, with one flip, we can increase the number of runs by at most 2.

And we can achieve an increase of 2 when possible.

In the example "000", we achieved increase of 2.

In "01" (n=2), initial runs=2
- After any flip, we can have at most 2 runs (because "01" has 2, "10" has 2, "11" has 1, "00" has 1)
- So maximum is 2, increase of 0.

So the maximum number of runs after one flip is:
- If the string is already alternating (1 or 2 runs), then we might not be able to increase
- In general, the maximum is min(n, original_runs + 2)

But is that always true?

For a string with 3 runs, can we get 5 runs? Only if n≥5.

For example, "010" with n=3: runs=3
- Can we get 5 runs? Only if n≥5, so impossible.
- So maximum is min(n, original_runs + 2)

For n=3, maximum runs is min(3, original_runs + 2)

In "010", original_runs=3, so min(3,5)=3

Can we get 3 runs? Yes.

Can we get more than 3? Only if we have more than 3 runs, impossible in n=3.

In "000", original_runs=1, min(3,1+2)=3 → and we can achieve 3 runs as shown.

In "01", n=2, original_runs=2, min(2,2+2)=2 → and we cannot achieve more than 2.

In "10000011", n=8, original_runs=3, min(8,3+2)=5 → matches expected output.

So the algorithm is:

1. Count the number of runs in the string
2. Answer = min(n, runs + 2)

But is this always achievable?

For any string, with one flip, can we always increase the number of runs by at most 2, and sometimes by 2?

In the case where the string is already alternating, can we increase?

For "0101", n=4, runs=4
- Can we increase to 6? Only if n≥6, so no
- Can we increase to 4+2=6? Only if n≥6
- So for n=4, max runs = min(4,4+2)=4

Can we achieve more than 4 runs in a string of length 4? No, because maximum is 4 (alternating).

So yes.

For a string like "0000", n=4, runs=1
- Can we get 3 runs? Yes, by flipping position 1: "0100" → runs: "0", "1", "00" → 3 runs
- Can we get 4 runs? "0101" has 4 runs, can we get that with one flip?
- Try flipping position 1: "0100" → 3 runs
- Try flipping position 2: "0010" → "00", "1", "0" → 3 runs
- Try flipping position 1 and 2: "0110" → "0", "11", "0" → 3 runs
- Try flipping position 1 and 3: is that a contiguous substring? only one substring per contiguous block.
- Try flipping [1,2]: "0010" → positions:0:0,1:1,2:1,3:0 → runs: "0", "11", "0" → 3 runs
- Try flipping [1,3]: "0111" → "0", "111" → 2 runs
- Try flipping [0,1]: "1100" → "11", "00" → 2 runs
- Can we get 4 runs? Only if we have pattern like 0,1,0,1
- How about flip position 1 only: "0100" → positions:0:0,1:1,2:0,3:0 → not alternating
- To get 0,1,0,1, we need s[0]=0, s[1]=1, s[2]=0, s[3]=1
- From "0000", we need to set s[1]=1, s[2]=0, s[3]=1, but only one contiguous flip.
- If we flip [1,3]: s[1]=1, s[2]=1, s[3]=1 → becomes "0111" → not 0,1,0,1
- If we flip [1,2]: s[1]=1, s[2]=1 → "0110" → not 0,1,0,1
- So cannot achieve 4 runs with one flip from "0000"

Maximum is 3 runs.

So min(4,1+2)=3 → matches.

Therefore, the maximum number of runs after one flip is min(n, initial_runs + 2)

So the solution is:

1. Count the number of runs in the input string
2. Output min(n, runs + 2)

Let me verify with examples.

Example 1: "10000011"
- Runs: "1", "00000", "11" → 3 runs
- min(8, 3+2) = min(8,5) = 5 → correct

Example 2: "01"
- Runs: "0", "1" → 2 runs
- min(2, 2+2) = min(2,4) = 2 → correct

Another example: "0000", n=4
- runs=1
- answer = min(4,1+2)=3

We saw that we can achieve 3 runs, so correct.

Edge: n=1, string "0"
- runs=1
- answer = min(1,1+2)=1
- Only possibility: flip the only character → "1" → still 1 run → correct

Edge: n=1, string "01" not possible, n=1 only one char.

Another: "010", n=3
- runs: "0", "1", "0" → 3 runs
- answer = min(3,3+2)=3 → correct, and we cannot have more than 3 runs in n=3.

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count number of runs
runs = 1
for i in range(1, n):
    if s[i] != s[i-1]:
        runs += 1

# Maximum possible runs after one flip
# It can increase by at most 2, and is at most n
result = min(n, runs + 2)
print(result)
```

Wait, in the example with "0000", we got 3, which is correct.

But in the for loop, when s[i] != s[i-1], we increment runs.

For "0000": all equal, so runs=1 initially, then no ++, so runs=1 → correct.

For "10000011": 
- i=1: s[1]='0', s[0]='1' → different → runs=2
- i=2: '0'=='0' → no
- i=3: '0'=='0' → no
- i=4: '0'=='0' → no
- i=5: '0'=='0' → no
- i=6: '1'!='0' → runs=3
- i=7: '1'=='1' → no
- So runs=3 → correct.

Therefore, the code is:

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count number of runs
runs = 1
for i in range(1, n):
    if s[i] != s[i-1]:
        runs += 1

# Maximum possible runs after one flip is min(n, runs + 2)
print(min(n, runs + 2))
```

I believe this is the correct solution. The reasoning is that with one flip, we can increase the number of runs by at most 2, and we can always achieve that increase in most cases (as shown in examples), and the number of runs cannot exceed n. Therefore, the answer is min(n, runs + 2). 

This matches both sample tests and the edge cases I've considered.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Kevin has just recevied his disappointing results on the USA Identification of Cows Olympiad (USAICO) in the form of a binary string of length n. Each character of Kevin's string represents Kevin's score on one of the n questions of the olympiad—'1' for a correctly identified cow and '0' otherwise.

However, all is not lost. Kevin is a big proponent of alternative thinking and believes that his score, instead of being the sum of his points, should be the length of the longest alternating subsequence of his string. Here, we define an alternating subsequence of a string as a not-necessarily contiguous subsequence where no two consecutive elements are equal. For example, {0, 1, 0, 1}, {1, 0, 1}, and {1, 0, 1, 0} are alternating sequences, while {1, 0, 0} and {0, 1, 0, 1, 1} are not.

Kevin, being the sneaky little puffball that he is, is willing to hack into the USAICO databases to improve his score. In order to be subtle, he decides that he will flip exactly one substring—that is, take a contiguous non-empty substring of his score and change all '0's in that substring to '1's and vice versa. After such an operation, Kevin wants to know the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that his string could have.

Input

The first line contains the number of questions on the olympiad n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000).

The following line contains a binary string of length n representing Kevin's results on the USAICO. 

Output

Output a single integer, the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that Kevin can create in his string after flipping a single substring.

Examples

Input

8
10000011


Output

5


Input

2
01


Output

2

Note

In the first sample, Kevin can flip the bolded substring '10000011' and turn his string into '10011011', which has an alternating subsequence of length 5: '10011011'.

In the second sample, Kevin can flip the entire string and still have the same score.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string of length n

3. We can flip exactly one contiguous substring (change 0s to 1s and 1s to 0s)
4. We want to maximize the length of the longest alternating subsequence after the flip

## Key Insights

1. In any binary string, the longest alternating subsequence is essentially the number of "transitions" between 0s and 1s plus 1.
2. For example, in "1010", we have transitions at every position, so the alternating subsequence length is 4.
3. In "1100", we have transitions only at position 2, so the alternating subsequence length is 2 (either "10" or "01").
4. The key insight is that we want to maximize the number of transitions in our string.

## Analysis of the Alternating Subsequence

For any binary string, the length of the longest alternating subsequence equals the number of "blocks" of consecutive identical characters plus 1.

For example:
- "10101" has blocks: [1], [0], [1], [0], [1] → 5 blocks → length 5
- "1100" has blocks: [11], [00] → 2 blocks → length 2
- "10001" has blocks: [1], [000], [1] → 3 blocks → length 3

So we need to maximize the number of blocks after flipping one contiguous substring.

I will code accordingly. 

Note: we must be careful to count runs correctly.

In a string, a run is a maximal sequence of consecutive identical characters.

Implementation: iterate through the string and group consecutive identical characters.

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count runs and find max run length
runs = 0
max_run_length = 0
i = 0
while i < n:
    j = i
    while j < n and s[j] == s[i]:
        j += 1
    run_length = j - i
    runs += 1
    max_run_length = max(max_run_length, run_length)
    i = j

# Determine the answer
if max_run_length >= 3:
    answer = runs + 2
elif max_run_length >= 2:
    answer = runs + 1
else:  # max_run_length == 1
    answer = runs

print(answer)
```

This should be the solution. It passes all provided examples and the additional ones I tested. 

The reasoning is that with one flip, we can always increase the number of runs by at least 1 if there is a run of length at least 2 (by flipping a part of a run of length 2), and by 2 if there is a run of length at least 3 (by flipping a part of a run of length at least 3 to create more transitions), and in the absence of such, we cannot increase beyond the current runs. 

The examples in the problem match, so I will submit this.

n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count runs and find max run length
runs = 0
max_run_length = 0
i = 0
while i < n:
    j = i
    while j < n and s[j] == s[i]:
        j += 1
    run_length = j - i
    runs += 1
    max_run_length = max(max_run_length, run_length)
    i = j

# Determine the answer
if max_run_length >= 3:
    answer = runs + 2
elif max_run_length >= 2:
    answer = runs + 1
else:  # max_run_length == 1
    answer = runs

print(answer)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Kevin has just recevied his disappointing results on the USA Identification of Cows Olympiad (USAICO) in the form of a binary string of length n. Each character of Kevin's string represents Kevin's score on one of the n questions of the olympiad—'1' for a correctly identified cow and '0' otherwise.

However, all is not lost. Kevin is a big proponent of alternative thinking and believes that his score, instead of being the sum of his points, should be the length of the longest alternating subsequence of his string. Here, we define an alternating subsequence of a string as a not-necessarily contiguous subsequence where no two consecutive elements are equal. For example, {0, 1, 0, 1}, {1, 0, 1}, and {1, 0, 1, 0} are alternating sequences, while {1, 0, 0} and {0, 1, 0, 1, 1} are not.

Kevin, being the sneaky little puffball that he is, is willing to hack into the USAICO databases to improve his score. In order to be subtle, he decides that he will flip exactly one substring—that is, take a contiguous non-empty substring of his score and change all '0's in that substring to '1's and vice versa. After such an operation, Kevin wants to know the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that his string could have.

Input

The first line contains the number of questions on the olympiad n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000).

The following line contains a binary string of length n representing Kevin's results on the USAICO. 

Output

Output a single integer, the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that Kevin can create in his string after flipping a single substring.

Examples

Input

8
10000011


Output

5


Input

2
01


Output

2

Note

In the first sample, Kevin can flip the bolded substring '10000011' and turn his string into '10011011', which has an alternating subsequence of length 5: '10011011'.

In the second sample, Kevin can flip the entire string and still have the same score.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string of length n
2. We want to find the longest alternating subsequence (where no two consecutive elements are equal)
3. We can flip exactly one contiguous substring (reverse 0s and 1s)
4. We need to find the maximum possible length of the longest alternating subsequence after one flip

## Key Insights

1. In any binary string, the longest alternating subsequence is essentially the number of "transitions" between 0 and 1, plus 1 (since we can always start from the first element).

2. For a binary string, the longest alternating subsequence length equals the number of positions where adjacent elements differ, plus 1.

3. More specifically: if we have a sequence, the alternating subsequence can be constructed by taking the first element, then taking every time the value changes from the previous one.

4. The maximum possible alternating subsequence length in any binary string is n (when the string alternates perfectly: 010101... or 101010...).

5. What happens when we flip a substring? We're essentially changing a contiguous segment of 0s and 1s to the opposite values.

6. The key insight is that flipping a substring can only change the alternating subsequence length by altering the transitions between positions.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Read n and the string
2. Count the number of contiguous blocks of identical characters
3. Output min(n, blocks + 2)

Let me verify with edge cases:

- n=1, "0": blocks=1, min(1,1+2)=1 → correct
- n=1, "1": blocks=1, min(1,3)=1 → correct
- n=2, "00": blocks=1, min(2,3)=2 → can we achieve 2? flip one of them: "10" or "01" → 2 blocks → yes

All seem correct.

So the final solution:

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count the number of blocks
blocks = 1
for i in range(1, n):
    if s[i] != s[i-1]:
        blocks += 1

# The maximum possible alternating subsequence length is min(n, blocks + 2)
result = min(n, blocks + 2)
print(result)
```

Wait, is blocks + 2 always achievable? In all our examples it seems to be.

But is there a case where we cannot achieve blocks + 2 because of parity or other constraints?

For example, if n is odd and the string starts and ends with the same character, can we create an alternating pattern?

In alternating patterns of length n, if n is odd, then first and last are the same. If n is even, first and last are different.

In our flip operation, when can we create an alternating pattern?

An alternating pattern has a specific parity: if it starts with 0, then positions: 0,2,4,... are 0; positions 1,3,5,... are 1. So in an odd-length alternating string, first and last are the same.

Similarly for starting with 1.

In a flip, we change a contiguous substring. The total number of 0s and 1s changes by 2*length of substring (since we flip each bit).

But more importantly, the parity of the number of 1s changes if the length is odd, remains the same if even.

For an alternating pattern of even length, it must have the same first and last character (if it starts with 0, ends with 0; if starts with 1, ends with 1).

For odd length, it starts and ends with the same character.

But with one flip, can we change the first and last character?

In the substring flip, if we flip a substring that includes the first or last position, and especially if it's only one position, then we can change the first or last character.

In particular, if we flip a single position at the end, we can change the last character.

So we can change the parity of the string at either end.

Therefore, we can achieve any alternating pattern with the same length as long as the length is at most n, and it has the correct parity for the first and last character.

But since we can choose any substring, we can always achieve an alternating pattern of length k for any k from 2 to n, as long as k is at most n.

For example, to achieve a pattern of length k, we can have a prefix of length i, then a transition, then a suffix of length k-i.

But with one flip, can we achieve any such pattern?

In our earlier examples, we could achieve up to blocks+2, and blocks+2 is always at most n when we take min(n, blocks+2).

And in the cases where blocks+2 > n, we take n.

So the formula min(n, blocks+2) gives the maximum possible block count.

Therefore, I believe this is correct.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count the number of contiguous blocks
blocks = 1
for i in range(1, n):
    if s[i] != s[i-1]:
        blocks += 1

# The maximum number of blocks after one flip is at most n, and at least blocks + 2 if possible, otherwise blocks
# But from examples, it's min(n, blocks + 2)
result = min(n, blocks + 2)
print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Kevin has just recevied his disappointing results on the USA Identification of Cows Olympiad (USAICO) in the form of a binary string of length n. Each character of Kevin's string represents Kevin's score on one of the n questions of the olympiad—'1' for a correctly identified cow and '0' otherwise.

However, all is not lost. Kevin is a big proponent of alternative thinking and believes that his score, instead of being the sum of his points, should be the length of the longest alternating subsequence of his string. Here, we define an alternating subsequence of a string as a not-necessarily contiguous subsequence where no two consecutive elements are equal. For example, {0, 1, 0, 1}, {1, 0, 1}, and {1, 0, 1, 0} are alternating sequences, while {1, 0, 0} and {0, 1, 0, 1, 1} are not.

Kevin, being the sneaky little puffball that he is, is willing to hack into the USAICO databases to improve his score. In order to be subtle, he decides that he will flip exactly one substring—that is, take a contiguous non-empty substring of his score and change all '0's in that substring to '1's and vice versa. After such an operation, Kevin wants to know the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that his string could have.

Input

The first line contains the number of questions on the olympiad n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000).

The following line contains a binary string of length n representing Kevin's results on the USAICO. 

Output

Output a single integer, the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that Kevin can create in his string after flipping a single substring.

Examples

Input

8
10000011


Output

5


Input

2
01


Output

2

Note

In the first sample, Kevin can flip the bolded substring '10000011' and turn his string into '10011011', which has an alternating subsequence of length 5: '10011011'.

In the second sample, Kevin can flip the entire string and still have the same score.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

1. We have a binary string of length n

4. We can flip exactly one contiguous substring (change all 0s to 1s and all 1s to 0s)

## Key Insights

1. In any binary string, the longest alternating subsequence is simply the number of "transitions" between 0 and 1 plus 1 (the first element).

   - Transitions: 1→0 (at position 0), then 0→1 (at position 5)

5. If the string already has a long alternating subsequence, we might not need to flip anything. But we're allowed to flip exactly one substring to maximize the result.

## Strategy

When we flip a substring, what happens to the number of runs?

Let's say we have the original string with r runs. We want to flip a substring from index i to j.

Before flipping, we have some runs. After flipping, the runs change at the boundaries.

The key insight: flipping a substring can only increase or decrease the number of runs by at most 2 (specifically, it can increase by at most 2 or decrease by at most 2).

But actually, let's think about it:
- When we flip a substring, the boundaries where the string changes from 0 to 1 or 1 to 0 will change.
- Specifically, at the start point (i-1 to i), if the character at i-1 is different from the new character at i, we get a new transition.
- Similarly, at the end point (j to j+1), if the character at j is different from the new character at j+1, we get a new transition.
- Inside the substring, we're flipping all 0s to 1s and 1s to 0s, so the internal transitions within the flipped part get inverted, but we also have to consider the junctions.

This is complex. Let me consider simpler cases.

Actually, there's a known result: the maximum number of runs achievable in a binary string after flipping one substring is either the original number of runs or can be increased by 2 at most.

But let me verify with examples:

Example 1: "10000011" 
- Original runs: 1 (1), 00000, 11 → 3 runs
- After flipping "00000" to "11111", we get "10111111"
- Runs: 1, 0, 111111 → 3 runs
- After flipping "0000" (positions 1-4) to "1111", we get "11111011" → runs: 11111, 0, 11 → 3 runs

Therefore, the solution is:
1. Count the number of runs in the original string
2. If the string is already alternating (every character differs from its neighbor), then answer = n
3. Otherwise, answer = min(n, original_runs + 2)

But is the "already alternating" condition sufficient?

In "0101", every adjacent pair differs, so it is alternating, and answer = n = 4.

So it works.

Therefore, the algorithm:

1. Read n and the string
2. Count the number of runs (maximal consecutive identical characters)
3. Check if the string is already alternating (every adjacent pair differs)
4. If yes, output n
5. If no, output min(n, runs + 2)

But wait, what about the alternating condition? 

In the alternating condition, we require that for every i from 0 to n-2, s[i] != s[i+1].

If this is true, then the number of runs is exactly n (since each character is different from the next), so we output n.

Otherwise, we output min(n, runs + 2)

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: "10000011"
- runs: 
  - '1' (1) → run1
  - '00000' (5) → run2
  - '11' (2) → run3
  - total runs = 3
- Check alternating: positions 0-1: '1','0' → different; 1-2: '0','0' → same → not alternating
- So output min(8, 3+2) = min(8,5) = 5 → matches

Example 2: "01"
- runs: '0', '1' → 2 runs
- Check alternating: positions 0-1: '0','1' → different → alternating
- So output n = 2 → matches

Another example: "00" 
- runs: '00' → 1 run
- alternating? 0,0 → same → not alternating
- output min(2, 1+2) = min(2,3) = 2

Can we achieve 2 runs from "00"?
- Flip any substring:
  - flip position 0: "10" → runs: '1','0' → 2 runs
  - flip position 1: "01" → runs: '0','1' → 2 runs
  - flip positions 0-1: "11" → runs: '11' → 1 run
- So maximum is 2 → correct.

Another example: "001"
- runs: '00','1' → 2 runs
- alternating? 0,0 → same → not alternating
- output min(3, 2+2) = min(3,4) = 3
- Can we achieve 3 runs? 
  - "010" has 3 runs
  - From "001", can we get "010" with one flip?
  - We need to flip a contiguous substring to change it.
  - Try flip positions 1-2: "0" + flip("0","1") → "0" + "1","0" = "010" → yes!
  - So achievable → correct.

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count the number of runs
runs = 1
for i in range(1, n):
    if s[i] != s[i-1]:
        runs += 1

# Check if the string is already alternating (every adjacent pair differs)
is_alternating = True
for i in range(n-1):
    if s[i] == s[i+1]:
        is_alternating = False
        break

if is_alternating:
    print(n)
else:
    print(min(n, runs + 2))
```

Wait, in the alternating case, runs = n, and we output n, which is correct.

In non-alternating case, we output min(n, runs + 2).

This matches all examples and the reasoning.

Let me double-check the "00000" example:
- n=5, s="00000"
- runs: only one run → 1
- is_alternating? no (0 and 0 at positions 0-1)
- output min(5, 1+2) = 3
- And we can achieve 3 runs, as shown.

Perfect.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Kevin has just recevied his disappointing results on the USA Identification of Cows Olympiad (USAICO) in the form of a binary string of length n. Each character of Kevin's string represents Kevin's score on one of the n questions of the olympiad—'1' for a correctly identified cow and '0' otherwise.

However, all is not lost. Kevin is a big proponent of alternative thinking and believes that his score, instead of being the sum of his points, should be the length of the longest alternating subsequence of his string. Here, we define an alternating subsequence of a string as a not-necessarily contiguous subsequence where no two consecutive elements are equal. For example, {0, 1, 0, 1}, {1, 0, 1}, and {1, 0, 1, 0} are alternating sequences, while {1, 0, 0} and {0, 1, 0, 1, 1} are not.

Kevin, being the sneaky little puffball that he is, is willing to hack into the USAICO databases to improve his score. In order to be subtle, he decides that he will flip exactly one substring—that is, take a contiguous non-empty substring of his score and change all '0's in that substring to '1's and vice versa. After such an operation, Kevin wants to know the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that his string could have.

Input

The first line contains the number of questions on the olympiad n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000).

The following line contains a binary string of length n representing Kevin's results on the USAICO. 

Output

Output a single integer, the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that Kevin can create in his string after flipping a single substring.

Examples

Input

8
10000011


Output

5


Input

2
01


Output

2

Note

In the first sample, Kevin can flip the bolded substring '10000011' and turn his string into '10011011', which has an alternating subsequence of length 5: '10011011'.

In the second sample, Kevin can flip the entire string and still have the same score.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string of length n
2. We want to find the longest alternating subsequence (where no two consecutive elements are equal)
3. We can flip exactly one contiguous substring (change 0s to 1s and 1s to 0s)
4. We need to find the maximum possible length of the longest alternating subsequence after one flip

## Key Insights

1. In any binary string, the longest alternating subsequence is simply the number of "transitions" between 0 and 1 plus 1 (the first element).

2. Actually, let me think more carefully: in an alternating subsequence, we can pick elements as long as adjacent ones in the subsequence are different. The maximum possible length is achieved when we pick every element that starts a new alternating segment.

3. For any binary string, the longest alternating subsequence has length = number of "runs" (consecutive identical characters) + 1.

4. Why? Because we can always pick one element from each run (starting with the first run), and we get exactly one element per run.

5. So the problem reduces to: find a contiguous substring to flip such that the number of runs in the resulting string is maximized.

6. When we flip a substring, we're essentially changing the pattern of runs. The key is to understand how flipping affects the run count.

## Strategy Analysis

Let me think about what happens when we flip a substring:

- Before flipping, we have some runs
- After flipping, runs at the boundaries of the flipped segment will change
- Specifically, at the boundaries where the flipped substring meets non-flipped parts, we might create or destroy transitions

The key insight is that flipping a substring can change the run count. We need to find which flip maximizes the run count.

Actually, let's think differently. The longest alternating subsequence is equal to the number of runs. So we want to maximize the number of runs.

When we flip a substring, what happens to the runs?

- If we flip a substring that is entirely within a single run, we just create a new run pattern, but the total number of runs might not increase much
- If we flip a substring that spans across multiple runs, we might create new transitions

But there's a simpler insight: in the optimal case, if we can create a string that alternates perfectly, like "010101...", we get a perfect alternating sequence.

The maximum possible alternating subsequence length is n (if we can make perfect alternation).

But we can only flip one contiguous substring.

Let me reconsider: the longest alternating subsequence in any binary string is equal to the number of runs.

So our goal is to maximize the number of runs after one flip.

## Key Observations

1. If the original string already has alternating runs (like "010101"), then it has n runs, so flipping won't increase it.

2. When we flip a substring, it can break existing runs or create new ones.

3. Flipping a substring of length k will affect the runs at the boundaries.

4. Actually, there's a pattern: flipping a substring can at most create two new transitions (at the left and right boundaries), but it can also destroy transitions if the flip creates identical adjacent elements.

Wait, let me think of a different approach.

## Alternative Insight

Let me consider what the answer should be:

- In the worst case, if we have all 0s, the longest alternating subsequence is 1 (we can only pick one 0)
- In the best case, if we can make the string alternate perfectly, we get n.

But can we always achieve perfect alternation with one flip?

For example, "10000011" → can we make it alternate?
Original runs: "1" (1), "00000" (1), "11" (1) → total 3 runs

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: "10000011"

- min(8, 3+2) = min(8,5) = 5 → matches example output

Example 2: "01"
- runs: '0' to '1' → change at index 0, so runs = 1 + 1 = 2
- min(2, 2+2) = min(2,4) = 2 → matches

Example: "0000" (n=4)
- runs: only one run (all 0s) → runs = 1
- answer = min(4,1+2) = 3 → as we saw, we can achieve 3

Example: "01" (n=2) → runs=2, answer=min(2,4)=2

Example: "010" (n=3) → runs: '0','1','0' → changes at 0→1 and 1→0 → 2 changes → runs = 3
- answer = min(3,3+2)=3 → correct

Example: "011" (n=3) → changes: 0→1 (change), 1→1 (no) → one change → runs = 2
- answer = min(3,2+2)=3 → and we can achieve 3 by flipping position 2 to get "010" → which has 3 runs

Therefore, the solution is:

1. Count the number of runs in the string (number of times the character changes from one position to the next, plus 1)
2. Answer = min(n, runs + 2)

Now I need to implement this.

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count the number of runs
if n == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    runs = 1  # at least one run
    for i in range(1, n):
        if s[i] != s[i-1]:
            runs += 1
    # The answer is min(n, runs + 2)
    answer = min(n, runs + 2)
    print(answer)
```

Let me double-check with the examples:

Example 1: "10000011"
- i=1: '0' != '1' → runs=2
- i=2-5: '0'=='0' → no change
- i=6: '1' != '0' → runs=3
- runs = 3
- answer = min(8, 3+2) = 5 → correct

Example 2: "01"
- i=1: '1' != '0' → runs=2
- answer = min(2,2+2)=2 → correct

Example: "0000" (n=4)
- no changes in any i from 1 to 3 → runs=1
- answer = min(4,1+2)=3 → correct

Example: "011"
- i=1: '1'!='0' → runs=2
- i=2: '1'=='1' → no change
- runs=2
- answer = min(3,4)=3 → correct

Seems all examples work.

Therefore, the final solution is:

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count the number of runs in the string
runs = 1
for i in range(1, n):
    if s[i] != s[i-1]:
        runs += 1

# The maximum possible length of the longest alternating subsequence after one flip
answer = min(n, runs + 2)
print(answer)
```

Each character of Kevin's string represents Kevin's score on one of the n questions of the olympiad—'1' for a correctly identified cow and '0' otherwise.

However, all is not lost. Kevin is a big proponent of alternative thinking and believes that his score, instead of being the sum of his points, should be the length of the longest alternating subsequence of his string. Here, we define an alternating subsequence of a string as a not-necessarily contiguous subsequence where no two consecutive elements are equal. For example, {0, 1, 0, 1}, {1, 0, 1}, and {1, 0, 1, 0} are alternating sequences, while {1, 0, 0} and {0, 1, 0, 1, 1} are not.

Kevin, being the sneaky little puffball that he is, is willing to hack into the USAICO databases to improve his score. In order to be subtle, he decides that he will flip exactly one substring—that is, take a contiguous non-empty substring of his score and change all '0's in that substring to '1's and vice versa. After such an operation, Kevin wants to know the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that his string could have.

Input

The first line contains the number of questions on the olympiad n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000).

The following line contains a binary string of length n representing Kevin's results on the USAICO. 

Output

Output a single integer, the length of the longest possible alternating subsequence that Kevin can create in his string after flipping a single substring.

Examples

Input

8
10000011


Output

5


Input

2
01


Output

2

Note

In the first sample, Kevin can flip the bolded substring '10000011' and turn his string into '10011011', which has an alternating subsequence of length 5: '10011011'.

In the second sample, Kevin can flip the entire string and still have the same score.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

- (4,5): 1=1 → no
- (5,6): 1=1 → no
- (6,7): 1=1 → no
- So only two transitions: at (0,1) and (1,2)

T_final = 2

Which is the same as T_initial = 2.

So in this case, no gain.

Perhaps for the example to work, we need to flip a different part.

Let me try flipping positions 1 to 5 in "10000011":
- positions: 1,2,3,4,5
- s[1]=0→1, s[2]=0→1, s[3]=0→1, s[4]=0→1, s[5]=0→1
- string: 1,1,1,1,1,1,1,1 → all 1s
- only one group → T_final = 0

No good.

Try flipping positions 0 to 1 in "10000011":
- positions 0,1: s[0]=1→0, s[1]=0→1
- string: 0,1,0,0,0,0,1,1
- transitions:
  - (0,1): 0≠1 → yes
  - (1,2): 1≠0 → yes
  - (2,3): 0=0 → no
  - (3,4): 0=0 → no
  - (4,5): 0=0 → no
  - (5,6): 0≠1 → yes
  - (6,7): 1=1 → no
- transitions at (0,1), (1,2), (5,6) → T_final = 3
- longest alternating subsequence = 4

But expected is 5.

Try flipping positions 3 to 5:
- original: positions 3,4,5: 0,0,0 → become 1,1,1
- string: 1,0,0,1,1,1,1,1
- positions: 0:1,1:0,2:0,3:1,4:1,5:1,6:1,7:1
- transitions:
  - (0,1): 1≠0 → yes
  - (1,2): 0=0 → no
  - (2,3): 0≠1 → yes
  - (3,4): 1=1 → no
  - (4,5): 1=1 → no
  - (5,6): 1=1 → no
  - (6,7): 1=1 → no
- transitions at (0,1), (2,3) → T_final = 2

No.

Try flipping positions 3 to 4:
- positions 3,4: 0,0 → become 1,1
- string: 1,0,0,1,1,0,1,1
- positions: 0:1,1:0,2:0,3:1,4:1,5:0,6:1,7:1
- (0,1):1≠0 → yes
- (1,2):0=0 → no
- (2,3):0≠1 → yes
- (3,4):1=1 → no
- (4,5):1≠0 → yes
- (5,6):0≠1 → yes
- (6,7):1=1 → no
- transitions at (0,1), (2,3), (4,5), (5,6) → 4 transitions → T_final = 4 → longest alternating subsequence = 5

Yes! So with this flip, we get length 5.

Now let's apply our boundary rule to this flip: l=3, r=4
- l>0: s[2]=0, s[3]=0 → equal → change +1
- r<7: s[4]=0, s[5]=0 → equal → change +1
- net change = +2
- T_initial = 2 (at positions 0 and 5)
- T_final = 2 + 2 = 4 → matches

Perfect! So our boundary change rule is correct.

Summary of the algorithm:

1. Calculate the initial number of adjacent differences T_initial.
2. For each possible substring [l,r] (l from 0 to n-1, r from l to n-1), compute the change in T due to flipping:
   - Δ = 0
   - If l > 0 and s[l-1] == s[l]: Δ += 1
   - If l > 0 and s[l-1] != s[l]: Δ -= 1
   - If r < n-1 and s[r] == s[r+1]: Δ += 1
   - If r < n-1 and s[r] != s[r+1]: Δ -= 1
   - (Note: internal transitions within [l,r] do not change)
3. The final T for this flip is T_initial + Δ
4. The longest alternating subsequence is T_final + 1
5. We want the maximum over all possible flips of (T_final + 1)

But n can be up to 100,000, and there are O(n^2) substrings, so we can't try all.

We need to optimize.

Notice that the change Δ only depends on l and r through:
- whether l>0 and s[l-1] == s[l]
- whether l>0 and s[l-1] != s[l]
- whether r< n-1 and s[r] == s[r+1]
- whether r< n-1 and s[r] != s[r+1]

So for each l, we have a value:
- left_gain = 1 if l>0 and s[l-1]==s[l] else -1 if l>0 and s[l-1]!=s[l] else 0

Similarly for each r, right_gain = 1 if r< n-1 and s[r]==s[r+1] else -1 if r< n-1 and s[r]!=s[r+1] else 0

Then for a substring [l,r], the change is: left_gain[l] + right_gain[r]

We want to maximize: T_initial + left_gain[l] + right_gain[r]

Since T_initial is constant, we want to maximize left_gain[l] + right_gain[r] over all 0≤l≤r≤n-1.

This is a classic optimization: we can iterate over l from 0 to n-1, and for each l, we want to choose r≥l to maximize right_gain[r].

But note: r must be ≥ l.

So for each l, we can precompute the maximum right_gain[r] for r ≥ l.

Let max_right[l] = max{ right_gain[r] for r from l to n-1 }

Then for each l, the maximum left_gain[l] + max_right[l] is a candidate.

Then overall maximum change is max over l of (left_gain[l] + max_right[l])

Then the answer is T_initial + max_change + 1

We must also consider the possibility that no flip is needed, but the problem says we must flip exactly one substring, so we are forced to do a flip.

But actually, we are to find the maximum possible after one flip, so we are to maximize over all flips.

Edge case: if we can't get a better result, but in our analysis, we are taking the maximum over all flips.

Let's test with the examples.

Example 1: "10000011", n=8
- T_initial: positions where s[i] != s[i+1]
  - i=0: 1!=0 → yes
  - i=1: 0==0 → no
  - i=2: 0==0 → no
  - i=3: 0==0 → no
  - i=4: 0==0 → no
  - i=5: 0!=1 → yes
  - i=6: 1==1 → no
  - so T_initial = 2

- For each position:
  - left_gain: for l=0: l>0? no → 0
  - l=1: s[0]=1, s[1]=0 → different → -1
  - l=2: s[1]=0, s[2]=0 → same → +1
  - l=3: s[2]=0, s[3]=0 → same → +1
  - l=4: s[3]=0, s[4]=0 → same → +1
  - l=5: s[4]=0, s[5]=0 → same → +1
  - l=6: s[5]=0, s[6]=1 → different → -1
  - l=7: l>0, s[6]=1, s[7]=1 → same → +1

  - right_gain: for r from 0 to 7:
    - r=0: r<7, s[0]=1, s[1]=0 → different → -1
    - r=1: r<7, s[1]=0, s[2]=0 → same → +1
    - r=2: s[2]=0, s[3]=0 → same → +1
    - r=3: s[3]=0, s[4]=0 → same → +1
    - r=4: s[4]=0, s[5]=0 → same → +1
    - r=5: s[5]=0, s[6]=1 → different → -1
    - r=6: r<7, s[6]=1, s[7]=1 → same → +1
    - r=7: r==7>=7, so r<7 false → 0

  - So right_gain = [-1, 1,1,1,1,-1,1,0]

  - max_right: from l to n-1
    - l=0: max of [-1,1,1,1,1,-1,1,0] = 1
    - l=1: max of [1,1,1,1,-1,1,0] = 1
    - l=2: [1,1,-1,1,0] = 1
    - l=3: [1,1,-1,0] = 1
    - l=4: [1,-1,0] = 1
    - l=5: [-1,1,0] = 1
    - l=6: [1,0] = 1
    - l=7: [0] = 0

  - Then for each l: left_gain[l] + max_right[l]
    - l=0: 0 + 1 = 1
    - l=1: -1 + 1 = 0
    - l=2: 1 + 1 = 2
    - l=3: 1 + 1 = 2
    - l=4: 1 + 1 = 2
    - l=5: 1 + 1 = 2
    - l=6: -1 + 1 = 0
    - l=7: 1 + 0 = 1

  - So maximum change = 2

  - Final T = 2 + 2 = 4
  - Longest alternating subsequence = 4 + 1 = 5

Perfect! Matches expected output.

Example 2: "01", n=2
- T_initial: i=0: 0!=1 → yes → T_initial=1
- left_gain:
  - l=0: 0
  - l=1: l>0, s[0]=0, s[1]=1 → different → -1
- right_gain:
  - r=0: r<1 (n-1=1), s[0]=0, s[1]=1 → different → -1
  - r=1: r==1, not <1 → 0
- max_right:
  - l=0: max of [-1,0] = 0
  - l=1: max of [0] = 0
- left_gain + max_right:
  - l=0: 0 + 0 = 0
  - l=1: -1 + 0 = -1
- max change = 0
- Final T = 1 + 0 = 1
- Longest alternating subsequence = 1 + 1 = 2

Matches expected output.

So the algorithm is:

1. Read n and the string s
2. Calculate T_initial = number of i from 0 to n-2 such that s[i] != s[i+1]
3. Precompute left_gain for each l in [0, n-1]:
   - left_gain[l] = 1 if l>0 and s[l-1]==s[l] else -1 if l>0 and s[l-1]!=s[l] else 0
4. Precompute right_gain for each r in [0, n-1]:
   - right_gain[r] = 1 if r < n-1 and s[r]==s[r+1] else -1 if r < n-1 and s[r]!=s[r+1] else 0
5. Precompute max_right[l] = max{ right_gain[r] for r from l to n-1 }
6. For each l from 0 to n-1, compute candidate = left_gain[l] + max_right[l]
7. max_change = max(candidate over l)
8. answer = T_initial + max_change + 1

Note: we must consider that r must be >= l, and we have precomputed max_right for r>=l.

Let me code accordingly.

Edge case: n=1
- T_initial = 0
- left_gain[0] = 0 (l>0 false)
- right_gain: for r=0, r<0? false → 0
- max_right[0] = 0
- max_change = 0
- answer = 0+0+1 = 1

For "0" or "1", after flipping (the only substring), we get the same string, so longest alternating subsequence is 1. Correct.

So it should work.

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Calculate initial number of adjacent differences
T_initial = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    if s[i] != s[i+1]:
        T_initial += 1

# Edge case: n == 1
if n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    # Compute left_gain for each l in [0, n-1]
    left_gain = [0] * n
    for l in range(1, n):
        if s[l-1] == s[l]:
            left_gain[l] = 1
        else:
            left_gain[l] = -1
    # left_gain[0] = 0 by initialization
    
    # Compute right_gain for each r in [0, n-1]
    right_gain = [0] * n
    for r in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        if s[r] == s[r+1]:
            right_gain[r] = 1
        else:
            right_gain[r] = -1
    # right_gain[n-1] = 0 by initialization
    
    # Compute max_right[l] = max{ right_gain[r] for r from l to n-1 }
    max_right = [0] * n
    max_right[n-1] = right_gain[n-1]
    for l in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        max_right[l] = max(right_gain[l], max_right[l+1])
    
    # Find maximum change over all l
    max_change = 0
    for l in range(n):
        change = left_gain[l] + max_right[l]
        if change > max_change:
            max_change = change
    
    result = T_initial + max_change + 1
    print(result)
```

s = input().strip()

# Calculate initial number of adjacent differences
T_initial = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    if s[i] != s[i+1]:
        T_initial += 1

# Edge case: n == 1
if n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    # Compute left_gain for each l in [0, n-1]
    left_gain = [0] * n
    for l in range(1, n):
        if s[l-1] == s[l]:
            left_gain[l] = 1
        else:
            left_gain[l] = -1
    # left_gain[0] = 0 by initialization
    
    # Compute right_gain for each r in [0, n-1]
    right_gain = [0] * n
    for r in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        if s[r] == s[r+1]:
            right_gain[r] = 1
        else:
            right_gain[r] = -1
    # right_gain[n-1] = 0 by initialization
    
    # Compute max_right[l] = max{ right_gain[r] for r from l to n-1 }
    max_right = [0] * n
    max_right[n-1] = right_gain[n-1]
    for l in range(n-2, -1, -1):
        max_right[l] = max(right_gain[l], max_right[l+1])
    
    # Find maximum change over all l
    max_change = 0
    for l in range(n):
        change = left_gain[l] + max_right[l]
        if change > max_change:
            max_change = change
    
    result = T_initial + max_change + 1
    print(result)
